\caption[The diagonal residue and the b-trace]{(a) The lifted diagonal
meets the front face at one point. Along \(\Delta_b\) the diagonal value
of the kernel of \(A\in x^{-\ell}\Psi_b^{-\infty}(X)\) has a Laurent
expansion in \(x\), and its constant coefficient is the inverse Fourier
transform of \(b_0\) at \(z=0\) \eqref{eq:diagvalue}. (b) The term \(x^k\)
contributes \(\varepsilon^{w+k}/(w+k)\) to \(\Tr(x^wA)\), so there are at
most simple poles at \(w=-k\), \(k\geq-\ell\) (Lemma~\ref{lem:residue}).
The residue at \(w=0\) is the boundary trace and the finite part is the
b-trace. For a commutator, \(\Tr(x^w[A,B])=w\,\Tr(x^wC(w)B)\) is regular
at \(0\), with value \(\sum_H\Tr_{\partial,H}(D(A)B)\)
(Theorem~\ref{thm:defect}).}
\label{fig:btrace}
\end{figure}

\begin{theorem}[b-trace defect]\label{thm:defect}
For \(A,B\in x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{-\infty}(X)\) with \(T_H(A)=\sum x^ka_k\) and
\(T_H(B)=\sum x^kb_k\),
\[
 {}^b\Tr([A,B])=\sum_H\Tr_{\partial,H}\bigl(D(A)B\bigr)
 =\sum_H\frac{i}{2\pi}\sum_k\int_{\mathbb R}
 \Tr_H\bigl((\partial_\lambda a_k)(\lambda+ki)\,b_{-k}(\lambda)\bigr)\,
 d\lambda .
\]
For \(A,B\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}(X)\) this is
\(\frac{i}{2\pi}\sum_H\int\Tr_H(\partial_\lambda I_H(A,\lambda)\,
I_H(B,\lambda))\,d\lambda\), which is the formula of
\cite[Thm.~``b-trace defect formula'']{Notes}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For \(\operatorname{Re}w\) large, \(x^wAB\), \(x^wBA\) and \(Ax^wB\) are
trace class (Q0), and their traces are integrals of their kernels over the
diagonal. Both \(\Tr(x^wBA)\) and \(\Tr(Ax^wB)\) equal the absolutely
convergent double integral \(\iint x(p)^wK_B(p,q)K_A(q,p)\,\mu_b\mu_b\). Hence
\[
 \Tr(x^w[A,B])=\Tr\bigl((x^wA-Ax^w)B\bigr)=w\,\Tr\bigl(x^wC(w)B\bigr),
 \qquad C(w)=\frac{A-x^{-w}Ax^{w}}{w}.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:conj}, \(C(w)\) has kernel \(w^{-1}(1-s^{-w})K_A\). This
is an entire family in \(x^{-\ell}\Psi_b^{-\infty}\) with \(C(0)=D(A)\).
By Lemma~\ref{lem:residue}, \(\Tr(x^wC(w)B)\) has at most a simple pole at
\(0\), with residue \(\sum_H\Tr_{\partial,H}(D(A)B)\). Hence
\(\Tr(x^w[A,B])\) is regular at \(0\), and its value there is that residue.
The explicit form follows from \eqref{eq:skew}: the constant term of
\((x^ki\partial_\lambda a_k)(x^{-k}b_{-k})\) is
\(\alpha^{-k}(i\partial_\lambda a_k)b_{-k}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Laurentres}
Let \(\mathcal M=x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{-\infty}(X)\). In the long exact sequence of
\(0\to\Ib\to\mathcal M\to\Idb\to0\), the composite
\(\Tr\circ\partial:\HH_1(\Idb)\to\C\) equals \(-\sum_H\varphi_H\).
Consequently \(\HH_0(\mathcal M)\cong\C^N\), spanned by the residues
\(\operatorname{Res}_{w=0}\Tr(x^w\,\cdot\,)\) at the components, and
\(\HH_1(\mathcal M)\cong\C^{N-1}\), while \(\HH_k(\mathcal M)=0\) for
\(k\geq2\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The ideal \(\Ib\) is H-unital (Lemma~\ref{lem:Hunital}), and
\(\Ib\) is a closed ideal of the filtered nuclear algebra
\(x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{-\infty}\), whose quotient is identified with \(\Idb\) by
Proposition~\ref{prop:structure}. So Theorem~\ref{thm:excision} applies. Let \(c=\sum a_i\otimes b_i\) be a
\(1\)-cycle, with lifts \(\tilde a_i,\tilde b_i\). Then
\(\partial[c]=[\sum[\tilde a_i,\tilde b_i]]\). Since \({}^b\Tr=\Tr\) on
\(\Ib\), Theorem~\ref{thm:defect} gives \(\Tr\partial[c]=\sum_{H,i}
\Tr_{\partial,H}(D(a_i)b_i)=-\sum_{H,i}\varphi_H(a_i,b_i)\), using the
cyclicity in Proposition~\ref{prop:generators}. This map
\(\C^N\to\C\) is surjective. Now insert this into the long exact sequence,
together with Proposition~\ref{prop:I} and Theorem~\ref{thm:Id}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Dependence on choices]\label{rem:choices}
The algebras and extensions depend only on \(X\), because
\(x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{\Z}\) and the ideals are unchanged under \(x\mapsto ax\)
with \(0<a\in C^\infty(X)\). The Taylor maps \(T_H\) depend on \(x\) and on
the collar. The residue \(\Tr_{\partial,H}=\operatorname{Res}_{w=0}
\Tr(x^w\,\cdot\,)\) does not: since \(a^w=1+w\log a+O(w^2)\), the extra
term \(w\Tr(x^w\log a\,A)\) is regular at \(0\). The b-trace changes by
\(\Tr_\partial(\log a\,\cdot\,)\). The derivation changes to
\(D'=D+[\log a,\cdot]\), so
\[
 \varphi'_H(A,B)=\varphi_H(A,B)+\Tr_{\partial,H}(A[\log a,B])
 =\varphi_H(A,B)-(b\psi)(A,B),\qquad\psi(A)=\Tr_{\partial,H}(A\log a),
\]
using the trace property. Hence the class of \(\varphi_H\) is
independent of the choices.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
Theorem~\ref{thm:Laurentres} is a homological form of the statement that
the b-trace is not a trace. The trace on \(\Ib\) admits no extension to a
trace on \(\mathcal M\), and the obstruction is exactly
\(\sum_H\varphi_H\). The traces of \(\mathcal M\) are the \(N\) boundary
residues.
\end{remark}

\part{Assembly and the index functional}\label{part:assembly}

\section{The interior ideal and the boundary symbol algebra}\label{sec:Is}

\subsection{Convergence and degeneration}\label{subsec:conv}
The algebras \(\Isb\) and \(\Adsb\) are computed by the same order spectral
sequence as \(\Asb\) in Part~\ref{part:symbols}; only the coefficient
functions change. In this subsection we separate the purely algebraic part
of the convergence question from the analytic one.

Let \((C_\bullet,b)\) be a complex such that each \(C_k\) is a
\emph{subspace} of the space of series \(c=\sum_{p\leq P}c_p\) with
\(c_p\in C_{p,k}\), containing every component \(C_{p,k}\), where \(b=\sum_{l\geq0}b_l\)
and \(b_l:C_{p,k}\to C_{p-l,k-1}\). Put \(F_pC_k=\{c:c_q=0\text{ for }q>p\}\),
let \(E^1_{p,k}=H_k(C_{p,\bullet},b_0)\), and let \(d^1\) be induced by
\(b_1\). Since \(\sum_{i+j=l}b_ib_j=0\), \(d^1\) is well defined and
\((d^1)^2=0\); let \(E^2\) be its homology. Because \(C_k\) is only a subspace, completeness is a real condition.
We call the filtration
\emph{complete} if every series \(\sum_je_j\) with \(e_j\in F_{p_j}C_k\),
\(\sup p_j<\infty\) and \(p_j\to-\infty\), defines an element of \(C_k\), and
\(b\) commutes with such sums.

\begin{proposition}[Algebraic convergence]\label{prop:algconv}
Assume the filtration is complete and that, for some integer \(n\),
\(E^2_{p,k}=0\) whenever \(p\neq k-n\). Then every class in \(H_k(C,b)\)
has a representative in \(F_{k-n}C_k\), and
\[
 \Theta:H_k(C,b)\to E^2_{k-n,k},\qquad[c]\mapsto[c_{k-n}],
\]
is an isomorphism. All differentials \(d^r\), \(r\geq2\), vanish.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is the argument of \cite[Ch.~``Hochschild homology'', convergence proposition]{MelroseMicro},
written out in our notation.

\emph{Step (P).} Let \(c\in F_pC_k\) with \(b_0c_p=0\) and
\(b_1c_p+b_0c_{p-1}=0\), and suppose \([c_p]=0\) in \(E^2_{p,k}\). Then
\([c_p]=d^1[x]\) for a \(b_0\)-cycle \(x\in C_{p+1,k+1}\), so
\(c_p-b_1x=b_0y\) with \(y\in C_{p,k+1}\). The components of \(b(x+y)\) of
degrees \(p+1\) and \(p\) are \(b_0x=0\) and \(b_1x+b_0y=c_p\), so
\(c-b(x+y)\in F_{p-1}\).

\emph{Representatives.} If \(c\in F_PC_k\) is a cycle and \(P>k-n\), then
\(E^2_{P,k}=0\), so (P) lowers \(P\). Finitely many steps reach
\(F_{k-n}\).

\emph{Well defined.} Let \(c=be\in F_{k-n}\) with \(e\in F_q\). If
\(q>k+1-n\), then (P) applies to \(e\), since
\(b_0e_q=(be)_q=0\), \(b_1e_q+b_0e_{q-1}=(be)_{q-1}=0\) and
\(E^2_{q,k+1}=0\). It replaces \(e\) by an element of \(F_{q-1}\) with the
same boundary. Once \(q\leq k+1-n\), we have
\(c_{k-n}=b_1e_{k+1-n}+b_0e_{k-n}\), so \([c_{k-n}]=d^1[e_{k+1-n}]=0\) in
\(E^2\).

\emph{Injective.} If \(bc=0\), \(c\in F_{k-n}\), and \([c_{k-n}]=0\), apply
(P) repeatedly; this is possible because \(E^2_{p,k}=0\) for \(p<k-n\).
It yields \(e_p\in F_{p+1}C_{k+1}\), \(p=k-n,k-n-1,\dots\), with
\(c-b(\sum_{p\geq P}e_p)\in F_{P-1}\). By completeness, \(c=b\sum_pe_p\).

\emph{Surjective.} Let \(x\in C_{k-n,k}\) be a \(b_0\)-cycle with
\(d^1[x]=0\). Then \(b_1x=-b_0y\) for some \(y\), and \(c^{(1)}=x+y\) has
\(bc^{(1)}\in F_{k-n-2}\). Inductively, if \(z=bc^{(r)}\in F_p\) with
\(p=k-n-r-1\), then \(z\) is a cycle and \(p\neq k-1-n\), so (P) gives
\(x',y'\) of degree \(\leq p+1<k-n\) with \(z-b(x'+y')\in F_{p-1}\). Set
\(c^{(r+1)}=c^{(r)}-x'-y'\). By completeness the limit \(c\) exists,
\(bc=0\), and \(\Theta[c]=[x]\).

\emph{Degeneration.} The differential \(d^r\) maps \(E^r_{p,k}\) to
\(E^r_{p-r,k-1}\). Source and target can both be nonzero only if
\(p=k-n\) and \(p-r=(k-1)-n\), that is, only if \(r=1\). Hence \(d^r=0\) for
\(r\geq2\).
\end{proof}

The full chain complex fails the completeness condition of
Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv}. For example,
\(r^j\otimes r^{-2j}\) has total order \(-j\), and the sum over
\(j\geq1\) has unbounded first-slot order. The per-factor stages are
also unstable under the boundary. Take an element
\(a\) with scalar principal symbol of order \(N>0\) whose square is
nonzero. At the associated graded level the boundary of \(a^{\otimes3}\)
has components \(2a^2\otimes a\) and \(-a\otimes a^2\) of respective
multidegrees \((2N,N)\) and \((N,2N)\). Thus no enlargement of the
per-factor stage makes it a subcomplex. For flat symbols one can take
the principal symbol supported in the interior. We instead use a chain
complex with a separate bound on the coefficient slot.

\begin{lemma}[A mixed complex for Laurent complete symbols]
\label{lem:mixed-symbols}
Let \(A\) be either \(\Asb\) or \(\Adsb\), and let \(B\subset A\)
be its subalgebra of symbols of order at most zero and without
boundary poles. For \(L,M\geq0\) write \(A_{L,M}\) for the subspace
of pole order at most \(L\) and symbol order at most \(M\). The
natural inclusion
\[
 C_k(B,A)=\varinjlim_{L,M} A_{L,M}\widehat\otimes B^{\widehat\otimes k}
 \longrightarrow C_k(A)
\]
is a quasi-isomorphism. Here \(C(B,A)\) uses the Hochschild differential
with coefficients in the \(B\)-bimodule \(A\). Every
\(A_{L,M}\widehat\otimes B^{\widehat\otimes k}\) is a subcomplex in all
degrees and is complete for sums of decreasing total order.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use full-symbol coordinates obtained by left quantization in
b-coordinate charts. A partition of unity in the left variable
and cutoffs equal to one at the diagonal give a quantization of
each homogeneous symbol that commutes with left multiplication
by scalar base functions. Use product charts and cutoffs near the
boundary. Successively subtracting the quantized principal symbols
extracts the sequence of homogeneous terms. Asymptotic summation
realizes any such sequence modulo smoothing, with continuous
coordinate maps at each finite order quotient. The complete-symbol
topology at fixed pole bound is the product topology of these
terms. The same construction in the collar commutes with Taylor
expansion at the boundary. We use (Q0) and (C3) for this calculus.
Put \(t=r^{-1}\). Take an elliptic complete symbol
\(T\) of order \(-1\), without poles, with principal symbol \(t\).
The symbolic parametrix is an inverse in the complete-symbol algebra
and has order \(1\) and no poles. For \(\Adsb\) take the Taylor
image of this construction. Conjugation by \(x^L\) preserves order and
smooth coefficients for every integer \(L\), by the b-composition
formula. Thus with \(u_{L,M}=x^{-L}T^{-M}\) one has
\[
 A_{L,M}=u_{L,M}B=B u_{L,M}.
\]
Indeed multiplication by \(u_{L,M}\) has the asserted bounds, and its
inverse on each side takes a symbol with those bounds into \(B\).
The right inverse calculation uses conjugation by \(x^L\). All these
maps are continuous on fixed stages by (C3). In particular each
\(A_{L,M}\) is a \(B\)-bimodule. The mixed Hochschild boundary preserves
the fixed coefficient stage. On this stage the full-symbol splitting
writes the chain space as a product of homogeneous tensor components,
using commutation of completed nuclear tensor products with countable
products. At each total order there are finitely many multidegrees,
since the coefficient has order at most \(M\) and every other slot
has order at most zero. Thus sums tending to minus infinity in total
order converge there, and the boundary commutes with them since it
does not increase total order.

For the comparison, use the augmented bar bimodule complexes
\[
 P_k=A\widehat\otimes B^{\widehat\otimes k}\widehat\otimes A,
 \qquad
 Q_k=A\widehat\otimes A^{\widehat\otimes k}\widehat\otimes A,
\]
where every appearance of \(A\) is completed on bounded stages
before taking their algebraic union. Their differentials multiply
consecutive slots, without the cyclic face, and the augmentation in
degree zero is multiplication. On the subcomplex of \(P\) whose
rightmost slot lies in \(Bu\) for a fixed \(u=u_{L,M}\), leave the
leftmost slot unrestricted. There is a contraction
\[
 h_k(a\otimes\beta\otimes b u)
   =(-1)^{k+1}a\otimes\beta\otimes b\otimes u,
 \qquad h_{-1}(a)=a u^{-1}\otimes u.
\]
Here \(\beta\) denotes the middle slots. The face that multiplies the
last two slots of \(h_k\) gives the identity. All earlier faces
cancel against \(h_{k-1}d\), including the augmentation when \(k=0\).
These identities extend from elementary tensors by continuity. The
rightmost-slot bound is preserved and each fixed bound on the
leftmost slot is sent into a bounded stage. In \(Q\) the same
contraction uses \(u=1\) with middle slots in \(A\).

For clarity, the augmented comparison is valid on the completions as
follows. The diagonal stages \(A_{N,N}\) split continuously inside
\(A_{N+1,N+1}\). In full-symbol coordinates first remove the top
order and then remove the lowest boundary coefficient of every
remaining symbol by extending that coefficient in a collar with a
cutoff. This gives a continuous projection on the smaller stage.
For formal boundary symbols remove that coefficient in the formal
series. The procedure uses only one boundary jet per homogeneous
coefficient, continuously in the product topology. It does not
require a continuous section for the entire Taylor map. Tensor
powers of these projections give splittings of the corresponding
nested generator stages of \(Q_k\), and the generator space is
their algebraic direct sum of successive increments.

The inclusion \(P\to Q\) is the identity on the augmentation.
Construct an inverse chain map recursively on the free generators
\(1\otimes\beta\otimes1\) of \(Q\) and extend \(A\)-bilinearly.
In degree zero use the identity. In degree \(k\), the already
constructed image of the differential of a generator is a cycle
in \(P_{k-1}\). On each generator increment that image has a common
rightmost bound, so apply the displayed contraction for that bound
to lift the cycle. Extend the resulting map by linearity on the
sum of increments. Each original stage involves finitely many
increments, hence has continuous image in a bounded stage. The
bilinear extension is continuous into a bounded stage by the
continuity of multiplication and the projective tensor property.
For either composite, construct a homotopy to the identity by the
same recursion: on generators subtract the homotopy already
applied to the differential from the difference of the two maps,
and lift the resulting cycle. For generators of \(P\) the entire
middle tensor power is a single Fr\'echet stage. This gives
stagewise continuous bimodule comparison maps and homotopies.

Finally send \(a\otimes\beta\otimes a'\) to
\(\tau(a\otimes\beta\otimes a')=a'a\otimes\beta\), and write
\(s(a\otimes\beta)=a\otimes\beta\otimes1\). For every
bimodule map \(D\) just constructed, \(\tau D=\tau D s\tau\):
move \(a'\) from the right to the left under \(\tau\), first on
elementary tensors and then by density. The bar boundary descends
under \(\tau\) to the Hochschild boundary, its last face becoming the
cyclic face. Thus the comparison and both homotopies descend, and
the induced map of the inclusion is the claimed mixed inclusion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Lifts in a fixed coefficient stage]
\label{lem:mixed-lifts}
Let \(A\) and \(B\) be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:mixed-symbols}.
Fix \(L\geq1\) and \(M\geq0\). In the mixed stage
\(A_{L,M}\widehat\otimes B^{\widehat\otimes k}\), step (P) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv} stays in that stage whenever
\(p\leq M-2\) and \(p\neq k-n\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The graded algebra of \(B\) is \(C^\infty(Y)[t]\) in the smooth
case and \(C^\infty(Z)[[x]][t]\) in the formal case, with
\(\deg t=-1\). The graded coefficient module of the mixed stage
is the free symmetric module generated by \(x^{-L}t^{-M}\).
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:smooth-HKR} to the graded stage, with that
generator shifting the weight by \(M\). In weights \(q\leq M-1\)
the resulting forms are exactly homogeneous forms of weight
\(q\) in
\[
 L_L^k=x^{-L}\Omega^k
 \quad\subset\quad W_L^k=x^{-L}\Omega_b^k.
\]
In the formal case ordinary forms and b-forms are interpreted by
their Taylor series. To see the radial bound, write a form as
\(r^q(\alpha+d\log t\wedge\beta)\) with \(\alpha,\beta\) on
the sphere bundle. The term involving \(d\log t\) uses
\(t^{-q-1}dt\), whose coefficient order is \(q+1\), while the
first term uses coefficient order \(q\). There is no additional
bound for either term if \(q\leq M-1\). The ordinary boundary
differential is \(dx\), so in the b-frame the coefficient of
\(dx/x\) must have one extra power of \(x\).

The first differential is \(i\delta_b\) by the same antisymmetrization
as in Section~\ref{sec:poisson}, with the first entry allowed in the
symmetric graded coefficient module. Both \(L_L\) and \(W_L\) are
preserved by \(\delta_b\). For the former this follows directly
from \eqref{eq:poisson-boundary}: the b-Poisson bracket is smooth
on ordinary smooth functions and sends
\(x^{-L}C^\infty\times C^\infty\) into \(x^{-L}C^\infty\).
For the latter use the smooth b-frame and
\eqref{eq:symplectic-duality}.

The quotient \(W_L/L_L\) consists of the leading \(x^{-L}\)
coefficient of the \(dx/x\) component at the boundary. It is
acyclic in the homogeneous Poisson complex. In a collar let
\(\xi\) be the homogeneous coefficient of \(dx/x\) in the
tautological form. Exterior multiplication by \(d\xi\) preserves
both spaces, and with the convention of
\eqref{eq:poisson-boundary} one has
\[
 \delta_b(d\xi\wedge\cdot)+(d\xi\wedge\cdot)\delta_b
    =\mathcal L_{x\partial_x}.
\]
Indeed \([\iota_{\pi_b},d\xi\wedge\cdot]=-\iota_{x\partial_x}\);
use \(d(d\xi)=0\) and Cartan's identity. In the smooth case a
collar cutoff equal to one near the boundary makes the exterior
multiplication global and does not affect the identity on the
quotient. The Lie derivative is multiplication by \(-L\) there.
The resulting contraction raises fiber weight by one.

Suppose now that \(\theta\in L_L^k\) has fiber weight \(p\) and
\(\delta_b\theta=0\), with \(p\neq k-n\). In \(W_L\) apply
\(*_b\), contract with the fiber Euler field, and divide by the
nonzero weight \(p+n-k\). By
\eqref{eq:symplectic-duality}, with its degree sign, this gives a
primitive \(\eta\) of weight \(p+1\) in \(W_L^{k+1}\).
Its quotient class is closed and thus has a quotient primitive of
weight \(p+2\), by the preceding contraction. Lift this primitive
in \(W_L\) at weight \(p+2\) by extending its boundary coefficient
in the collar (or inserting its formal coefficient), and subtract
its \(\delta_b\) from \(\eta\). The resulting primitive lies in
\(L_L^{k+1}\) and has weight \(p+1\). The temporary lift is in
the form complex \(W_L\) and is not a chain-stage lift.

In step (P), the HKR class of \(c_p\) is \(\delta_b\)-closed by
the equation at order \(p-1\). The primitive just constructed,
divided by \(i\), is the class of a \(b_0\)-cycle \(x\) in the
same stage at order \(p+1\), since \(p+1\leq M-1\).
The difference \(c_p-b_1x\) is a \(b_0\)-boundary in the same
stage at order \(p\), again by the fixed-stage HKR calculation.
This gives \(y\) at that order. The fixed stage is a subcomplex,
so the remainder stays there.
\end{proof}

These two lemmas imply the convergence conclusion of
Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv} for the full mixed complexes. In
the direct limit, the \(E^1\)-page is the full Laurent or formal
Laurent form complex, since each homogeneous form belongs to some
\(L_L\) with arbitrarily large radial bound. Its \(E^2\) is
concentrated at \(p=k-n\) by Propositions~\ref{prop:E2}
and~\ref{prop:Ads}. The finite order reductions in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv} require no completeness and can be
done in the direct limit. For injectivity, perform the initial
step (P) at \(p=k-n\) there and choose a stage containing the
remainder, enlarging \(M\) so that all subsequent orders are
\(\leq M-2\) and taking \(L\geq1\). For surjectivity, choose the
initial two terms in the direct limit, then choose the coefficient
stage containing them and enlarge \(M\) in the same way. All
subsequent steps are off the critical row and stay in the one
stage by Lemma~\ref{lem:mixed-lifts}. The infinite sums converge
there by Lemma~\ref{lem:mixed-symbols}. The mixed inclusions
preserve total order and give the same HKR map on leading
symbols. The finite well-definedness argument of
Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv} applies in the ordinary chain
model as well, by its \(E^1\)- and \(E^2\)-calculations.
Consequently the isomorphism with \(E^2\) in the ordinary model
is also the leading-symbol map. This construction is natural
under the Taylor map from the smooth to the formal symbols.

\begin{lemma}[The flat symbol ideal]
\label{lem:mixed-flat}
The same leading-symbol isomorphism holds for \(J=\Isb\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First \(J\) is topologically H-unital in the chain model.
Choose a smooth locally finite partition of unity \((\psi_j)\)
on the interior of \(X\), using a dyadic decomposition of a
boundary collar and one compactly supported interior term.
For collar terms \(x\asymp 2^{-j}\) on the support. Choose
\(\phi_j\) supported in a slightly larger comparable region
and equal to one on \(\operatorname{supp}\psi_j\).
On the cofinal stages of nonnegative upper symbol order define
\[
 E(a_0\otimes c)=\sum_j\phi_j\otimes(\psi_j a_0)\otimes c .
\]
The smooth multiplication operators \(\phi_j,\psi_j\) have
order zero and are supported in the interior. In the full-symbol
topology, each fixed seminorm of \(\phi_j\) grows at most as a
power of \(2^j\). Each fixed flat-symbol seminorm of
\(\psi_j a_0\) is bounded by \(O(2^{-jK})\) times a stronger
flat-symbol seminorm of \(a_0\), for arbitrary \(K\). Indeed
the cutoff derivatives cost fixed powers of \(2^j\), and
flatness gives an arbitrarily high power of \(x\) on the
support. These estimates give convergence and continuity in
each projective tensor stage, without increasing order.
Associativity and \(\sum_j\phi_j\psi_j=1\) on the interior
give \(d_0 E=1\) and \(d_{i+1}E=Ed_i\), hence
\(b'E+Eb'=1\). By excision (Q1), \(C(J)\) maps
quasi-isomorphically to the kernel of the ordinary Taylor
map on chains.

Take also the kernel of the Taylor map between the two mixed
complexes. These Taylor maps are surjective on chains on
every fixed pair of coefficient bounds, since Taylor is
surjective on the fixed symbol stages and completed nuclear
tensor products preserve the quotients. They are surjective
also on associated graded pieces, by the homogeneous
coefficient description. The mixed inclusions give a
quasi-isomorphism between the mixed kernel and the ordinary
kernel by their homology exact sequences. At each fixed
mixed stage the \(b_0\)-homology of the kernel is the kernel
of Taylor on the fixed-stage HKR forms: the Taylor map
on those forms is surjective, by coefficientwise Borel
extension. This kernel consists of flat forms, and for
weights \(q\leq M-1\) it is the full space of flat forms of
weight \(q\). The Poisson primitive obtained from \(*_b\)
and the fiber Euler contraction is flat and needs no
boundary correction. Thus the same fixed-stage descent
applies to the mixed kernel. The \(E^2\)-page is as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:Is}. On associated graded complexes
the inclusion of \(C(J)\) into the ordinary kernel is the
flat HKR identification, by the exact sequence on graded
chains and the surjectivity on forms. These filtered maps
therefore identify the leading-symbol maps as well.
Equivalently, the finite reduction and well-definedness
arguments of Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv} apply directly
to \(C(J)\), and its homology is identified via the two
kernel comparisons with the homology computed by fixed
stages. This gives the asserted isomorphism in the
original flat chain model.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Two de Rham lemmas}

\begin{lemma}[Flat forms]\label{lem:flat}
Let \(V\) be a compact manifold with boundary, and let
\(\dot\Omega^*(V)\) be the complex of forms vanishing to infinite order at
\(\partial V\). Then \(H^*(\dot\Omega^*(V))\cong H^*(V,\partial V)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(J^*\) be the complex of formal Taylor series at \(\partial V\) of
forms on \(V\). In a collar these are
\(\sum_{k\geq0}x^k(a_k+dx\wedge c_k)\) with \(a_k,c_k\) forms on
\(\partial V\). By Borel's lemma,
\[
 0\to\dot\Omega^*(V)\to\Omega^*(V)\to J^*\to0
\]
is exact. On \(J^*\) put \(K(\sum x^k(a_k+dx\wedge c_k))=\sum
x^{k+1}c_k/(k+1)\), which is the formal integral from \(0\). The
fundamental theorem of calculus gives \(dK+Kd=1-\pi^*\rho\), where \(\rho\)
restricts to \(x=0\) and \(\pi\) is the collar projection. Hence
\(\rho:J^*\to\Omega^*(\partial V)\) is a quasi-isomorphism. The relative
complex \(\Omega^*(V,\partial V)=\Omega^*(V)\oplus\Omega^{*-1}(\partial V)\),
with \(D(\alpha,\beta)=(d\alpha,\alpha|_{\partial V}-d\beta)\), receives the
chain map \(\alpha\mapsto(\alpha,0)\) from \(\dot\Omega^*(V)\). This map is
compatible with the long exact sequences of the displayed short exact
sequence and of the pair \((V,\partial V)\). The five lemma gives the
claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Formal Laurent forms]\label{lem:formal}
Let \(W\) be a manifold, and let \(\Omega^*(W)((x))\) be formal Laurent
series in \(x\) with coefficients in forms on \(W\), together with the
generator \(dx/x\). The complex
\(\Omega^*(W)((x))\oplus\frac{dx}{x}\wedge\Omega^{*-1}(W)((x))\) has
cohomology \(H^*(W)\oplus\frac{dx}{x}H^{*-1}(W)\cong H^*(W\times
S_\partial)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(\mathcal E_x=x\partial_x\). It preserves the \(x\)-degree \(k\), and
on degree \(k\) we have \(d\iota_{\mathcal E_x}+\iota_{\mathcal E_x}d=
\mathcal L_{\mathcal E_x}=k\). For \(k\neq0\) the map
\(k^{-1}\iota_{\mathcal E_x}\) is a contracting homotopy. These homotopies
can be summed over \(k\), because they do not lower the \(x\)-degree. In
degree \(0\) the complex is
\(\Omega^*(W)\oplus\frac{dx}{x}\wedge\Omega^{*-1}(W)\) with
\(d(\alpha_0+\frac{dx}{x}\wedge\alpha_1)=d\alpha_0-\frac{dx}{x}\wedge
d\alpha_1\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The \(E^2\)-pages}{The E2-pages}}

\begin{proposition}[Interior ideal]\label{prop:Is}
\(E^2_{p,q}(\Isb)=H^{2n-p-q}(Y\times S_\sigma,Z\times S_\sigma)\) for
\(q=n\), and \(E^2_{p,q}(\Isb)=0\) otherwise. Moreover,
\(\HH_k(\Isb)\cong H^{2n-k}(Y\times S_\sigma,Z\times S_\sigma)\). The ideal
\(\Isb\) is the same algebra for the b- and cusp calculi.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The arguments of Sections~\ref{sec:graded}--\ref{sec:homogeneity} apply
verbatim to symbols that vanish to infinite order at \(x=0\), because
\(\pi_b\), \(*_b\) and the Euler field preserve flatness. The weight-zero
complex is \(\dot\Omega^*(Y\times S_\sigma)\), and Lemma~\ref{lem:flat}
computes its cohomology. The rows \(q\neq n\) vanish as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:E2}, so the spectral sequence degenerates. Kernels
conormal to the diagonal that vanish to infinite order at \(\partial(X^2)\)
do not depend on the blow-up, so \(x^\infty\Psi_b^{\Z}=x^\infty\Psi_c^{\Z}\).
Lemma~\ref{lem:mixed-flat} identifies this page with homology.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Boundary symbol algebra]\label{prop:Ads}
\(E^2_{p,q}(\Adsb)=H^{2n-p-q}(Z\times S_\sigma\times S_\partial)\) for
\(q=n\), and \(E^2_{p,q}(\Adsb)=0\) otherwise. Moreover,
\(\HH_k(\Adsb)\cong H^{2n-k}(Z\times S_\sigma\times S_\partial)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Elements of \(\Adsb\) are formal Laurent series in \(x\) of complete b-symbols
at \(\partial X\). On the \(E^1\)-page the coefficients are formal Laurent
series in \(x\) with values in homogeneous forms on
\({}^bT^*_{\partial X}X\setminus0\). The b-Poisson tensor
\(\pi_b=x\partial_x\wedge\partial_\xi+\sum\partial_{y_j}\wedge\partial_{\eta_j}\)
and the duality \(*_b\) preserve the \(x\)-degree, so \(d^1\) and the
symplectic duality of Section~\ref{sec:poisson} apply degree by degree.
The fibre Euler field kills the nonzero fibre weights
(Section~\ref{sec:homogeneity}); the weight-zero part produces
\(S_\sigma\). Lemma~\ref{lem:formal}, with \(W=Z\times S_\sigma\), kills
the nonzero \(x\)-degrees and produces \(S_\partial\). The row argument of
Proposition~\ref{prop:E2} gives degeneration.
The fixed coefficient stages of Lemmas~\ref{lem:mixed-symbols}
and~\ref{lem:mixed-lifts} give the leading-symbol isomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[\(n=1\)]\label{rem:n1}
Let \(n=1\). Then \(Y\) is two copies of \(X\) (\(\xi=\pm1\)), and each
\(Z_H\) is two points with the orientation of \(\partial Y\).
Parts (iv)--(vii) of Theorem~\ref{thm:full} hold as stated, with
\(\int_{Z_H}\) the signed sum over the two points. In (viii) the
kernel of the signed-sum map from \(H^0(Z)\cong\C^{2N}\) gives an
additional summand in degree one. Concretely:
\begin{itemize}
\item \(\HH_1(\Ab)\cong\C\oplus H^1(Y\times S_\sigma)\oplus
\ker\bigl(H^0(Z)\to\C^N\bigr)\);
\item \(\HH_2(\Ab)\cong\ker\bigl(H^0(Y)\to\C^N\bigr)\), where the map is
\(\beta\mapsto(\int_{Z_H}\beta)_H\);
\item for \(k=1,2\), \(\HH_k(\Adb)\) is the kernel of the surjection
\(\HH_k(\Adsb)\to\C^N\) given by the same signed sums.
\end{itemize}
For \(n=1\) the surjectivity of the connecting maps of \(\Adb\) and of
\(\partial^{\mathcal A}_1\) is proved by explicit test cycles
(Propositions~\ref{prop:n1} and~\ref{prop:connA}(d)).
For \(X=[0,1]\) with global coordinate \(s\), label the two copies
\(Y_\pm\) by the sign of the covector relative to \(ds\). They have
orientations \(\pm ds\). The map from \(H^0(Y)=\C^2\) to \(\C^2\)
of oriented periods is
\((c_+,c_-)\mapsto(c_--c_+,\,c_+-c_-)\) in endpoint order.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:description}, the trace-dual connecting
map is \((2\pi)^{-1}\) times this map. It has rank one and kernel
the constants, so \(\HH_2(\Ab)\cong\C\).
\end{remark}

\section{\texorpdfstring{The indicial algebra \(\Adb\)}{The indicial algebra}}\label{sec:Ad}

\subsection{The regularized suspended trace}
Let \(a\in B'_H\) have order \(m\) and put \(d=\dim H=n-1\). For \(p>m+d\)
the operator \(\partial_\lambda^pa(\lambda)\) has order \(m-p<-d\), so it is
trace class. On \(\mathbb R\) the function
\(f_p(\lambda)=\Tr_H\,\partial_\lambda^pa(\lambda)\) has a classical
expansion \(f_p\sim\sum_jc_j^\pm|\lambda|^{m-p+d-j}\) as
\(\lambda\to\pm\infty\). To see this, apply the trace formula
\(\Tr P=(2\pi)^{-d}\iint\sigma_P\,dy\,d\eta\) to the suspended symbol and
substitute \(\eta=|\lambda|\eta'\) in each homogeneous term. Let \(g_p\) be the
\(p\)-fold iterated integral of \(f_p\) from \(0\). Then
\(\int_{-T}^Tg_p\,d\lambda\) has an asymptotic expansion in powers \(T^j\)
and terms \(T^j\log T\) as \(T\to\infty\). Define \(\overline\Tr(a)\) as
\(1/2\pi\) times its constant term.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:bartr}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(1)] \(\overline\Tr(a)\) does not depend on \(p\). If \(a\) has order
\(<-n\), then \(\overline\Tr(a)=\frac1{2\pi}\int\Tr_Ha\,d\lambda\).
\item[(2)] \(\overline\Tr([a,b])=0\) for \(a,b\in B'_H\).
\item[(3)] If \(a\in B_H\), then \(\overline\Tr(\partial_\lambda^pa)=0\) for
\(p\geq1\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(1) Since \(g_{p+1}^{(p+1)}=f_{p+1}=f_p'=g_p^{(p+1)}\), the difference
\(g_{p+1}-g_p\) is a polynomial. The constant term of
\(\int_{-T}^T\lambda^j\,d\lambda\) is zero for every \(j\). For order
\(<-n\), \(g_p\) differs from \(\Tr_Ha\) by its Taylor polynomial at \(0\),
and \(\Tr_Ha\) is integrable.
(2) Here \(f_p([a,b])=\sum_j\binom pj\Tr_H[\partial^ja,\partial^{p-j}b]\).
Each term vanishes, because \(\Tr(PQ)=\Tr(QP)\) for pseudodifferential
operators on a closed manifold whose orders add up to less than \(-d\).
(3) follows from (1), since \(\int\partial_\lambda(\Tr\,\partial^{p-1}a)
\,d\lambda=0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Shift defect]\label{lem:shift}
Let \(a\in B'_H\) have order \(m\), let \(c\in\C\), and let \(P>m+n\). Then
\(a_c(\lambda)=a(\lambda+c)\) lies in \(B'_H\), and
\[
 \overline\Tr(a_c)-\overline\Tr(a)
 =\sum_{p=1}^{P-1}\frac{c^p}{p!}\,\overline\Tr(\partial_\lambda^pa).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Taylor's formula,
\(a_c=\sum_{p<P}\frac{c^p}{p!}\partial^pa+R\), where
\(R(\lambda)=\frac{c^P}{(P-1)!}\int_0^1(1-t)^{P-1}(\partial^Pa)
(\lambda+tc)\,dt\). The family \(\partial^Pa\) has order \(<-n\), uniformly
on strips, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:bartr}(1) we have
\(\overline\Tr(R)=\frac1{2\pi}\int\Tr R\,d\lambda\). A contour shift on the
strip turns \(\int\Tr(\partial^Pa)(\lambda+tc)\,d\lambda\) into
\(\int\partial_\lambda\Tr(\partial^{P-1}a)\,d\lambda\). This vanishes,
because \(\partial^{P-1}a\) has order \(<-d\) and its trace tends to \(0\)
at \(\pm\infty\).
\end{proof}

For \(A\in\Adb\) with \(T_H(A)=\sum x^ka_k\), put
\(\widehat\Tr_{\partial,H}(A)=\overline\Tr(a_0)\). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:bartr}(1), this extends \(\Tr_{\partial,H}\) from \(\Idb\).

\begin{proposition}[The connecting cocycle]\label{prop:omega}
For \(A,B\in\Adb\) with \(T_H(A)=\sum x^ka_k\) and \(T_H(B)=\sum x^kb_k\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:omega}
 \omega_H(A,B):=\widehat\Tr_{\partial,H}([A,B])
 =\sum_{k\neq0}\sum_{p\geq1}\frac{(ki)^p}{p!}\,
 \overline\Tr\bigl(\partial_\lambda^p(a_k\,\alpha^k(b_{-k}))\bigr),
\end{equation}
a finite sum. It vanishes if \(A\) or \(B\) lies in \(\Idb\), so it is a
Hochschild \(1\)-cocycle on \(\Adsb\). In the long exact sequence of
\(0\to\Idb\to\Adb\to\Adsb\to0\) we have
\(\Tr_{\partial,H}\circ\partial=\langle\omega_H,\cdot\rangle\) on
\(\HH_1(\Adsb)\). The cocycle
\[
 \Omega_H(A,B,C)=-\omega_H(A\,D(B),C)
\]
on \(\Adsb\) satisfies \(\varphi_H\circ\partial=\langle\Omega_H,\cdot\rangle\)
on \(\HH_2(\Adsb)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:skew}, the constant term of \([A,B]\) is
\(\sum_k(\alpha^{-k}(a_k)b_{-k}-\alpha^k(b_{-k})a_k)\). Put
\(F_k=a_k\alpha^k(b_{-k})\). Then \(\alpha^{-k}(F_k)=\alpha^{-k}(a_k)b_{-k}\),
and \(\overline\Tr(\alpha^k(b_{-k})a_k)=\overline\Tr(F_k)\) by
Lemma~\ref{lem:bartr}(2). Since \(\alpha^{-k}F(\lambda)=F(\lambda+ki)\),
Lemma~\ref{lem:shift} gives \eqref{eq:omega}. If \(a_k\in B_H\) or
\(b_{-k}\in B_H\), then \(F_k\in B_H\), and each term vanishes by
Lemma~\ref{lem:bartr}(3). The ideal \(\Idb\) is H-unital, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:conn} applies with \(\tau=\Tr_{\partial,H}\) and
\(\hat\tau=\widehat\Tr_{\partial,H}\).

For \(\varphi_H\), take \(\hat\varphi(A,B)=\widehat\Tr_{\partial,H}(AD(B))\).
It extends \(\varphi_H\), because \(D\) preserves \(\Idb\). A direct
expansion gives \(b\hat\varphi(A,B,C)=-\widehat\Tr([AD(B),C])\), which
vanishes when an argument lies in \(\Idb\). Lemma~\ref{lem:conn} then
gives the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Nonvanishing for \(n=1\)]\label{prop:n1}
Let \(n=1\), so that each \(H\) is a point and \(B'_H\) is the algebra of
entire functions with classical symbol expansions on strips. Let
\(\pi_+(\lambda)=\frac12(1+\operatorname{erf}\lambda)\in B'_H\), and work
in the summand of \(H\). Then
\[
 c_H=x^{-1}\pi_+\otimes x,\qquad
 c'_H=x^{-1}\pi_+\otimes h\otimes x-x^{-1}\pi_+\otimes x\otimes h
\]
are cycles in \(\Adsb\), and
\(\langle\omega_{H'},c_H\rangle=-\frac{i}{2\pi}\delta_{HH'}\) and
\(\langle\Omega_{H'},c'_H\rangle=-\frac1{2\pi}\delta_{HH'}\). Hence both
connecting maps \(\HH_1(\Adsb)\to\HH_0(\Idb)\) and
\(\HH_2(\Adsb)\to\HH_1(\Idb)\) are surjective.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:skew}, \((x^{-1}\pi_+)x=\alpha(\pi_+)\) and
\(x(x^{-1}\pi_+)=\pi_+\). Hence \(bc_H=\pi_+(\lambda-i)-\pi_+(\lambda)\).
This lies in \(\cE\), since \(\operatorname{erf}\) tends to \(\pm1\) on
strips with Gaussian error, so \(c_H\) is a cycle in \(\Adsb\). In
\eqref{eq:omega} only \(k=-1\) occurs, with \(F_{-1}=\pi_+\). Here
\(\overline\Tr(\pi_+')=\frac1{2\pi}\), while
\(\overline\Tr(\pi_+^{(p)})=0\) for \(p\geq2\). Therefore
\(\omega_H(c_H)=-i/2\pi\).

For \(c'_H\), use \(hx=x(h-i)\) and \(hx^{-1}=x^{-1}(h+i)\), which follow
from \eqref{eq:skew}. A direct computation gives
\(bc'_H=(\pi_+-\alpha(\pi_+))\otimes h\), which is zero in \(\Adsb\). Since
\(D(h)=i\) and \(D(x)=0\),
\(\Omega_H(c'_H)=-\omega_H(i\,x^{-1}\pi_+,x)=-\frac1{2\pi}\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{The connecting maps for \texorpdfstring{\(n\geq2\)}{n>=2}}
Identify the suspended variable \(\lambda\) with the b-covariable \(\xi\);
this is legitimate because \(xD_x\) has symbol \(\xi\) and indicial family
\(\lambda\). Then principal symbols of elements of \(B'_H\) are homogeneous
functions on \({}^bT^*_HX\setminus0=(T^*H\times\mathbb R_\xi)\setminus0\).
Write \(\zeta=(\eta,\xi)\) and \(d=n-1=\dim H\), and put
\[
 \hat\rho_H(g)=(2\pi)^{-n}\int_H\int_{\mathbb R^d}\bigl(g(y,\eta,1)-
 g(y,\eta,-1)\bigr)\,d\eta\,\nu_\partial(y)
\]
for \(g\) homogeneous of degree \(1-n\), with \(d\eta\) the density dual to
\(\nu_\partial\). The integrand is \(O(|\eta|^{-n})\), so the integral
converges.

\begin{lemma}[Residue formula]\label{lem:rhohat}
Let \(G\in B'_H\). If \(G\) has order \(\leq-n\), then
\(\overline\Tr(\partial_\lambda^pG)=0\) for \(p\geq1\). If \(G\) has order
\(1-n\) with principal symbol \(g\), then
\(\overline\Tr(\partial_\lambda G)=\hat\rho_H(g)\) and
\(\overline\Tr(\partial_\lambda^pG)=0\) for \(p\geq2\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If the order is \(\leq-n\), then \(\partial^pG\) has order \(<-n\), so
\(\overline\Tr(\partial^pG)=\frac1{2\pi}\int\partial_\lambda\Tr(\partial^{p-1}
G)\,d\lambda=0\) by Lemma~\ref{lem:bartr}. Now let \(G\) have order
\(1-n=-d\). The case \(p\geq2\) is the previous one applied to
\(\partial G\). Changing \(G\) by a family of order \(-n\) does not change
\(\overline\Tr(\partial G)\). Since \(\overline\Tr\) is defined for
suspended families on the real line, and its definition does not use
holomorphy, we may take \(G=\operatorname{Op}(\chi g)\) for real
\(\lambda\), quantized in charts, with \(\chi\) a cut-off near \(\zeta=0\).
This family is not entire. From the
definition of \(\overline\Tr\) applied to \(a=\partial G\) (order \(-n\)) with
\(p=1\): here \(g_1(\lambda)=\Tr\,\partial G(\lambda)-\Tr\,\partial G(0)\), and the
term \(2T\Tr\,\partial G(0)\) has no constant part. Hence
\(\overline\Tr(\partial G)\) is \(\frac1{2\pi}\) times the constant term of
\(I(T)=\int_{-T}^T\Tr\,\partial_\lambda G\,d\lambda\). Since
\(\partial_\lambda G\) has order \(-n<-d\), its trace is the integral of its
symbol, \(\Tr\,\partial_\lambda G=(2\pi)^{-d}\iint\partial_\lambda(\chi g)\,
d\eta\,\nu_\partial\). This is absolutely integrable over
\(\{|\lambda|\leq T\}\times\mathbb R^d\). By Fubini,
\[
 I(T)=(2\pi)^{-d}\int_H\int_{\mathbb R^d}\bigl(g(\eta,T)-g(\eta,-T)\bigr)\,
 d\eta\,\nu_\partial
\]
for \(T\) large. Substituting \(\eta=T\eta'\) and using homogeneity of
degree \(-d\) shows that \(I(T)\) is independent of \(T\), and equal to
\((2\pi)\hat\rho_H(g)\).
\end{proof}

For a chain \(c\) of \(\Adsb\), let \(\chi(c)\) be its HKR form, computed on
principal symbols. Let \(\theta_x\) and \(\theta_{\xi x}\) denote the
coefficients of \(dx/x\) and of \(d\xi\wedge dx/x\) in the \(x\)-weight-zero
part of a form \(\theta\).

\begin{lemma}[Top-order formulas]\label{lem:top}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] \(\omega_H\) vanishes on \(1\)-chains of total order \(\leq-n\).
On \(1\)-chains of total order \(\leq1-n\),
\(\omega_H(c)=-i\,\hat\rho_H\bigl(\chi(c_{1-n})_x\bigr)\).
\item[(b)] \(\Omega_H\) vanishes on \(2\)-chains of total order
\(\leq1-n\). For \(2\)-chains \(c\) of total order
\(\leq2-n\) with \(b_0c_{2-n}=0\),
\(\Omega_H(c)=-\hat\rho_H\bigl(\chi(c_{2-n})_{\xi x}\bigr)\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) In \eqref{eq:omega} the term \(F_k=a_k\alpha^k(b_{-k})\) has order at most
the total order, and \(\alpha^k\) does not change principal symbols.
Lemma~\ref{lem:rhohat} gives
\(\omega_H(A\otimes B)=\sum_kik\,\hat\rho_H(\sigma(a_k)\sigma(b_{-k}))\)
in total order \(1-n\), and \(0\) below. On the other hand
\(A\,dB=\sum x^ka_k\,d(x^{-k}b_{-k})\) has weight-zero \(dx/x\)-coefficient
\(-\sum_kk\,a_kb_{-k}\).

(b) The derivation \(D\) lowers order by one (Lemma~\ref{lem:conj}), so \(\Omega_H(A,B,C)=
-\omega_H(AD(B),C)\) is computed by (a). On elementary tensors this gives
\(\Omega_H(a\otimes b\otimes c)=-\sum m\,\hat\rho_H(a_j\partial_\xi b_lc_m)\),
summed over \(j+l+m=0\). Put \(P=\partial_\xi\), \(E=x\partial_x\),
and \(\ell=\hat\rho_H\circ[x^0]\) on symbols of order \(1-n\).
On a top elementary tensor \(a\otimes b\otimes c\), the difference
between \(\Omega_H\) and \(-\ell(\chi_2(\cdot)_{\xi x})\) is
\(-\tfrac12\ell(a(Pb\,Ec+Eb\,Pc))\). Set \(L(a,b)=\ell(aPEb)\).
The derivation rules for \(P,E\) and commutativity of the graded
product give \(b_0L(a,b,c)=-\ell(a(Pb\,Ec+Eb\,Pc))\).
Thus the difference is \(\tfrac12 b_0L\), which vanishes on
top \(b_0\)-cycles. These identities extend to completed chains by
continuity on the fixed graded pieces.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The boundary connecting maps]
\label{prop:dict}
Let \(n\geq1\), and assume the inputs of Theorem~\ref{thm:full}(ii)
and (Q1) for the indicial extension. Then both connecting maps
\(\HH_1(\Adsb)\to\HH_0(\Idb)\) and \(\HH_2(\Adsb)\to\HH_1(\Idb)\) are
surjective onto \(\C^N\). They are given on \(E^2\) by the functionals
\(\theta\mapsto-i\hat\rho_H(\theta_x)\) and
\(\theta\mapsto-\hat\rho_H(\theta_{\xi x})\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:omega}, Lemma~\ref{lem:top}, and the isomorphism
\(\Theta\) of Proposition~\ref{prop:algconv}, the composites
\(\Tr_{\partial,H}\circ\partial_1\) and \(\varphi_H\circ\partial_2\) are the
functionals \(\theta\mapsto-i\hat\rho_H(\theta_x)\) and
\(\theta\mapsto-\hat\rho_H(\theta_{\xi x})\) on \(E^2\). We exhibit
\(\delta_b\)-closed forms on which these are nonzero. Fix a metric on
\(H\), let \(|\zeta|^2=|\eta|^2+\xi^2\), and let \(\alpha_H=\eta\cdot dy\) be
the tautological form. Put
\[
 f=1_H\,\xi|\zeta|^{-n},\qquad
 \theta_1=f\,\frac{dx}{x}+1_H|\zeta|^{-n}\alpha_H,\qquad
 \theta_2=f\,d\xi\wedge\frac{dx}{x}+1_H|\zeta|^{-n}\,d\xi\wedge\alpha_H .
\]
These have fibre weights \(1-n\) and \(2-n\) and \(x\)-weight \(0\). With
\(\{a,c\}_b=\pi_b(da,dc)\), \eqref{eq:poisson-boundary} gives
\(\{f,\log x\}_b=-\partial_\xi f\), \(\{h,y_j\}_b=-\partial_{\eta_j}h\),
\(\{f,\xi\}_b=0\) and \(\{\xi,\log x\}_b=-1\). Hence
\[
 \delta_b\theta_1=-\operatorname{div}_\zeta V,\qquad
 \delta_b\theta_2=(\operatorname{div}_\zeta V)\,d\xi,\qquad
 V=\zeta|\zeta|^{-n}.
\]
Since \(\zeta\) ranges over \(\mathbb R^n\setminus0\), the field \(V\) is
divergence free:
\(\operatorname{div}V=n|\zeta|^{-n}-n|\zeta|^{-n-2}|\zeta|^2=0\). This holds
in any coordinates, because
\[
 \sum_j\eta_j\partial_{\eta_j}|\zeta|^2+\xi\partial_\xi|\zeta|^2=2|\zeta|^2 .
\]
So \(\theta_1\) and \(\theta_2\) define
\(E^2\)-classes. Now \((\theta_1)_x=(\theta_2)_{\xi x}=f\), and
\[
 \hat\rho_{H'}(f)=\delta_{HH'}\,2(2\pi)^{-n}\int_H\int_{\mathbb R^d}
 (1+|\eta|^2)^{-n/2}\,d\eta\,\nu_\partial\neq0 .
\]
This proves surjectivity.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Boundary periods]\label{prop:description}
Use the trace and cocycle coordinates of Theorem~\ref{thm:full}(ii).
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:dict}, the two boundary
connecting maps are
\[
 \beta\longmapsto -i(2\pi)^{-n}\Bigl(\int_{Z_H}\beta_\partial\Bigr)_H,
 \qquad
 \beta\longmapsto (2\pi)^{-n}\Bigl(\int_{Z_H}\beta_0\Bigr)_H .
\]
Here the boundary generator is written on the left, as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:formal}. The orientations of \(Y\) and \(Z_H\) are
given by \(\iota_{\mathcal E}\mu_b\) on \(r=1\) and the induced
boundary orientation, respectively.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On the component \(H\) write \(\vartheta=dx/x\) and
\(v=(d\eta\wedge dy)^d/d!\), where the power is that of the tangential
symplectic form. Thus \(\mu_b=d\xi\wedge\vartheta\wedge v\).
We have \(\pi_b^\sharp\vartheta=-\partial_\xi\) and
\(\pi_b^\sharp d\xi=x\partial_x\). Take the \(x\)-weight-zero
part of the HKR form \(\theta\), as allowed by Lemma~\ref{lem:formal}.
In degree one put \(g=\theta_x\) and let \(\alpha\) be the
coefficient of \(\vartheta\) in \(*_b\theta\). In degree two put
\(g=\theta_{\xi x}\) and let \(\alpha\) be the part of \(*_b\theta\)
free of \(\vartheta\). The form \(\alpha\) on
\((T^*H\times\mathbb R_\xi)\setminus0\) has degree \(2d\) and is
closed and invariant under radial dilation. Indeed, the star takes
Poisson cycles to closed forms of weight zero, and the two parts
of the formal \(x\)-weight-zero complex are closed separately.
The star sends \(g\vartheta\) to \(-g\vartheta\wedge v\) and
\(g\,d\xi\wedge\vartheta\) to \(gv\). In the chosen part of the
star all other terms contain \(d\xi\), since their contracting
multivector does not contain \(\partial_\xi\). Thus
\[
 \alpha|_{\xi=\pm1}=s\,g(y,\eta,\pm1)v,\qquad
 s=-1\ \text{in degree one},\quad s=1\ \text{in degree two}.
\]

Choose \(r^2=\xi^2+|\eta|^2\) in the collar and let \(t=\xi/r\)
on \(Z_H\). In radial coordinates write
\(\alpha=\gamma+d\log r\wedge\gamma_\sigma\), with both coefficients
pulled back from \(Z_H\) and closed there. The class of \(\gamma\)
is \(\beta_\partial\) in degree one and \(\beta_0\) in degree two.
The radial map from \(\xi=\pm1\) identifies
\(|\eta|\leq(\varepsilon^{-2}-1)^{1/2}\) with the cap
\(\pm t\geq\varepsilon\). The inverse map pulls \(\alpha\) back to
\(\gamma-d\log|t|\wedge\gamma_\sigma\), since \(r=1/|t|\) on
that hyperplane. On either cap the integral of the second term is
\[
 -\int_{\partial\{\pm t\geq\varepsilon\}}
            \log|t|\,\gamma_\sigma
 =-\log\varepsilon\int_{\partial\{\pm t\geq\varepsilon\}}
             \gamma_\sigma=0
\]
by Stokes and \(d\gamma_\sigma=0\). This controls the logarithmic
term before taking the limit. The paired hyperplane integrand also
converges absolutely: homogeneity gives
\(g(y,\eta,1)-g(y,\eta,-1)=\int_{-1}^1\partial_\xi
g(y,\eta,\xi)\,d\xi=O(|\eta|^{-n})\).

At the boundary the contraction in the outward normal direction gives
\(\iota_{-x\partial_x}\iota_{\mathcal E}\mu_b
=-\iota_{\mathcal E}(d\xi\wedge v)\).
The radial projection pulls \(\iota_{\mathcal E}(d\xi\wedge v)\)
on \(r=1\) back to \(r^{-n}\xi v\) on \(\xi=\pm1\). The
positive cap therefore has orientation \(-v\) in its hyperplane
coordinates and the negative cap has orientation \(v\). Adding the
oriented cap integrals and letting \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) gives
\(\int_{Z_H}\gamma=-s(2\pi)^n\hat\rho_H(g)\).
Combining this with Lemma~\ref{lem:top} gives the stated constants.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Ad}
Under the inputs of Theorem~\ref{thm:full}(ii) and (Q1) for the
indicial extension,
\(\HH_k(\Adb)\cong\HH_k(\Adsb)\) for \(k\neq1,2\). For \(k=1,2\), the group
\(\HH_k(\Adb)\) is the kernel of the surjection of
Proposition~\ref{prop:n1} (\(n=1\)), resp.\ Proposition~\ref{prop:dict}.
Proposition~\ref{prop:description} identifies the maps with the boundary periods.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the long exact sequence of Theorem~\ref{thm:excision} together with
Theorem~\ref{thm:Id}. Surjectivity of
\(\HH_2(\Adsb)\to\HH_1(\Idb)\) makes \(\HH_1(\Idb)\to\HH_1(\Adb)\) zero.
Surjectivity of \(\HH_1(\Adsb)\to\HH_0(\Idb)\) makes
\(\HH_0(\Idb)\to\HH_0(\Adb)\) zero. For \(k\geq3\) the groups of \(\Idb\)
vanish.
\end{proof}

\section{\texorpdfstring{The algebra \(\Ab\) and the full operator algebra}{The algebra Ab and the full operator algebra}}\label{sec:A}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:fibre}
\(\Isb\cap\Idb=0\) in \(\Ab\), and \(\Ab\to\Adb\times_{\Adsb}\Asb\) is an
isomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\(x^\infty\Psi_b^{\Z}\cap x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{-\infty}=x^\infty\Psi_b^{-\infty}\).
For any ring and ideals \(I,J\), the ring \(R/(I\cap J)\) is the fibre product
\(R/I\times_{R/(I+J)}R/J\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:connA}
Let \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_k:\HH_k(\Asb)\to\HH_{k-1}(\Idb)\) be the connecting
maps of \(0\to\Idb\to\Ab\to\Asb\to0\), and let \(p:\Asb\to\Adsb\) be the
quotient map.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_k=\partial^{\partial}_k\circ p_*\), where
\(\partial^\partial\) denotes the connecting maps of \(\Adb\).
\item[(b)] The composite \(\HH_1(\Idb)\to\HH_1(\Ab)\to\HH_0(\Ib)\), where
the second map is the connecting map of
\(0\to\Ib\to x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{\Z}\to\Ab\to0\), equals
\(-\sum_H\varphi_H\).
\item[(c)] The image of \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_2\) is contained in
\(\ker\sum_H\varphi_H\), so its rank is at most \(N-1\).
\item[(d)] If \(n=1\), then \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_1\) is surjective.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) and (b) express the naturality of the excision sequence for the
morphisms of extensions \((\Idb\subset\Ab)\to(\Idb\subset\Adb)\) and
\((\Ib\subset x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{-\infty})\to(\Ib\subset x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{\Z})\).
Part (b) then follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:Laurentres}.

(c) The composite \(\HH_2(\Asb)\to\HH_1(\Idb)\to\HH_1(\Ab)\) is zero by
exactness, and the second map followed by the connecting map of (b) is
\(-\sum\varphi_H\).

(d) Choose \(P_H\in\Psi_b^0(X)\), supported near \(H\), with full symbol
\(\chi_H(x)1_{\xi>0}\) and with no lower-order terms and with \(\chi_H=1\) near \(H\).
Then \(x^{-1}P_Hx-P_H\)
is smoothing, because the full symbol of \(x^{-1}Px\) is
\(\sum_k\frac{(-i)^k}{k!}\partial_\xi^kp\), by Lemma~\ref{lem:conj} applied
to symbols, and \(\partial_\xi p=0\) for \(\xi\neq0\). Hence
\(x^{-1}P_H\otimes x\) is a cycle in \(\Asb\). Its image under \(p\)
is the cycle \(c_H\) of Proposition~\ref{prop:n1} in the quotient:
the full Taylor symbol is constant in \(x\) near \(H\), and its
constant term is the complete symbol of \(\pi_+(\lambda)\). Here
\(\xi\) is the symbol of \(xD_x\), whose indicial family is \(\lambda\). Thus
\(\Tr_{\partial,H'}\partial^{\mathcal A}_1[x^{-1}P_H\otimes x]=
-\frac{i}{2\pi}\delta_{HH'}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Several boundary components]\label{rem:MNcomponents}
Take compatible collars and fibre norms in the symbol algebras.
On associated graded chains \(p\) takes the boundary Taylor series
in each slot and commutes with HKR and symplectic duality.
In the face splitting of Proposition~\ref{prop:face} the smooth
summand contributes its tangential restriction: a factor \(dx\)
has positive \(x\)-weight in the b-frame. The residue summand is
represented near the face by \(dx/x\) wedged on the left with its
boundary form. Thus after the contraction of the nonzero
\(x\)-weights the map in degree \(j=2n-k\) is
\[
 (a,b)\longmapsto a|_{Z\times S_\sigma}+d\theta_\partial\wedge b
 \quad\text{for }(a,b)\in H^j(Y\times S_\sigma)\oplus
 H^{j-1}(Z\times S_\sigma).
\]
By Proposition~\ref{prop:connA}(a), Proposition~\ref{prop:description},
and the naturality of \(\Theta\), the map \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_2\) is
\(\beta\mapsto(2\pi)^{-n}(\int_{Z_H}\beta)_H\) on the summand \(H^{2n-2}(Y)\) of
\(\HH_2(\Asb)\), and it vanishes on the other summands. By Stokes' theorem,
\(\sum_H\int_{Z_H}\beta=\int_Yd\beta=0\), which recovers the bound of
Proposition~\ref{prop:connA}(c) (Figure~\ref{fig:periods}). Exactness of
\(H^{2n-2}(Y)\to H^{2n-2}(\partial Y)\to H^{2n-1}(Y,\partial Y)\), together
with Lefschetz duality \(H^{2n-1}(Y,\partial Y)\cong H_0(Y)\) (C3), shows
that the image is the kernel of the sum map
\(H^{2n-2}(\partial Y)\to H_0(Y)\). If \(X\) is connected and \(n\geq2\),
then \(Y\) is connected, \(H^{2n-2}(\partial Y)=\C^N\), and the rank is
\(N-1\). If \(X\) has \(c\) components, each with nonempty boundary, the rank is
\(N-c\), and \(\operatorname{coker}\partial^{\mathcal A}_2\cong\C^c\). In the cusp case
Melrose and Nistor find \(\partial[i_{\log x}\Tr_\partial]=0\)
\cite[Prop.~12]{MN}, because \(d\theta_\sigma\) extends over the whole
cosphere bundle. For each \(H\) separately, the class
\(d\theta_\sigma\cdot1_{Z_H}\) does not extend when \(N\geq2\); only the
sum over all \(H\) does. We mention this only for comparison.
\end{remark}
