\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:full}}]
(i) is Proposition~\ref{prop:I}, (ii) is Theorem~\ref{thm:Id}, and (iii) is
Theorem~\ref{thm:Laurentres}. (iv) and (vi) are
Propositions~\ref{prop:Is} and~\ref{prop:Ads}, and (v) is
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}. (vii) is Theorem~\ref{thm:Ad}, with the components
identified by Proposition~\ref{prop:description}. For (viii), insert
\(\partial^{\mathcal A}_1=\partial^\partial_1\circ p_*\) and
\(\partial^{\mathcal A}_2\) into the long exact sequence. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:description} and the naturality of the top-component
map \(\Theta\), \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_1\) is
\(\beta\mapsto-i(2\pi)^{-n}(\int_{Z_H}\beta)_H\) on the summand
\(H^{2n-2}(Z)\cong\C^N\) of \(\HH_1(\Asb)\) and vanishes on the other
summands. The rank of \(\partial^{\mathcal A}_2\) is \(N-1\)
(Remark~\ref{rem:MNcomponents}). Use also \(\HH_*(\Idb)\)
(Figure~\ref{fig:LESA}). This gives \(\HH_0(\Ab)\cong\HH_0(\Asb)\),
\(\HH_1(\Ab)\cong\operatorname{coker}\partial^{\mathcal A}_2\oplus
\ker\partial^{\mathcal A}_1\), and \(\HH_2(\Ab)\cong\ker\partial^{\mathcal
A}_2\). (ix) follows from Corollary~\ref{cor:IF} and the long exact
sequence of \(0\to\Ib\to x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{\Z}\to\Ab\to0\), using (i).
\end{proof}

\section{The index functional}\label{sec:index}

\begin{lemma}[Inverses of indicial families]\label{lem:autoinv}
Let \(a\in B'_H\) have order \(m\) and be elliptic. If \(a(\lambda)\) is
invertible for every \(\lambda\in\C\), then \(a^{-1}\in B'_H\). If
\(a=1+k\) with \(k\in B_H\) and \(a(\lambda)\) is invertible for every
\(\lambda\in\C\), then \(a^{-1}-1\in B_H\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix a strip \(S=\{|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|\leq C\}\). The suspended
calculus (C3) gives \(q\in\Psi^{-m}_{\mathrm{sus}}\) on \(S\) with
\(aq=1-r\), where \(r\) is smoothing and decreases rapidly, in all
Sobolev norms, as \(|\operatorname{Re}\lambda|\to\infty\). The family
\(q\) need not be holomorphic. For \(|\operatorname{Re}\lambda|\geq R\)
the Neumann series gives \(a^{-1}=q(1-r)^{-1}=q+q\,r(1-r)^{-1}\). For
\(|\operatorname{Re}\lambda|\leq R\), the identity \(a^{-1}=q+a^{-1}r\)
holds with \(a^{-1}\) holomorphic, hence bounded on that compact set. So
\(a^{-1}-q\) is smoothing with the decay of \(r\), and \(a^{-1}\) is entire
because \(a\) is. Hence \(a^{-1}\in B'_H\). In the second case, \(a^{-1}\)
is uniformly bounded on each \(H^s\) over \(S\): use the Neumann series
where \(\|k\|<\tfrac12\), and compactness elsewhere. Then
\(a^{-1}-1=-k+ka^{-1}k\) is smoothing and decreases rapidly on \(S\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:invertible}
Let \(A\in\Psi_b^m(X)\) be b-elliptic. If for every \(H\) and every
\(\lambda\in\C\) the operator \(I_H(A,\lambda)\) is invertible, then
\([A]\) is invertible in \(\Ab\).
Conversely, if \([A]\) is invertible in \(\Ab\), then \(I_H(A,\lambda)\) is
invertible for every \(\lambda\in\C\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:fibre} it suffices to invert \(A\) in \(\Asb\), where the
usual symbolic parametrix construction works, and in \(\Adb\). Write
\(T_H(A)=\sum_{k\geq0}x^ka_k\) with \(a_0=I_H(A)\). By \eqref{eq:skew}, the
coefficient of \(x^k\) in \(T(A)\sum x^lb_l\) is
\(\alpha^k(a_0)b_k+\sum_{j\geq1}\alpha^{k-j}(a_j)b_{k-j}\). Solve recursively,
using \(\alpha^k(a_0)^{-1}=\alpha^k(a_0^{-1})\in B'_H\)
(Lemma~\ref{lem:autoinv}). A left inverse is
constructed in the same way, and the two inverses agree. Conversely, let
the inverse be \(\sum_{l\geq-\ell}x^lb_l\). Its lowest coefficient
satisfies \(b_{-\ell}a_0=0\). Since \(a_0(\lambda)\) is invertible for large
\(|\operatorname{Re}\lambda|\), analytic continuation gives
\(b_{-\ell}=0\) if \(\ell>0\). Hence \(b_0a_0=a_0b_0=1\) on all of \(\C\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Dirac operators]
A b-Dirac operator of product type, \(c(dx/x)(x\partial_x+A_\partial)\), has
\(I(\lambda)=c(dx/x)(i\lambda+A_\partial)\), which fails to be invertible at
\(\lambda=i\mu\) for every \(\mu\in\operatorname{spec}A_\partial\). It is
therefore never invertible in \(\Ab\). Its index on \(x^\delta L^2_b\) depends
on \(\delta\), and the algebra \(\Ab\) cannot see this dependence. This is
the precise form of the ``problems with completeness'' that led
Melrose--Nistor to the cusp calculus \cite[p.~3]{MN}, where indicial
families need only be invertible on \(\mathbb R\).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Fedosov]\label{prop:fedosov}
Let \(A\in x^{-\ell}\Psi_b^m(X)\) and \(B\in x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{\Z}(X)\) with
\(AB-1,BA-1\in\Ib\). Then
\(A:x^\delta H^s_b\to x^{\delta-\ell}H^{s-m}_b\) is Fredholm for all
\(\delta,s\), with kernel and cokernel in \(\dot C^\infty(X)\) and index
independent of \(\delta\) and \(s\). Moreover
\[
 \operatorname{Ind}(A)=\Tr(AB-BA)=-\Tr\bigl(\partial[B\otimes A]\bigr),
\]
where \(B\otimes A\) is a \(1\)-cycle of \(\Ab\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Operators in \(\Ib\) have kernels in \(\dot C^\infty(X^2)\), so they are
trace class on every weighted Sobolev space and map into
\(\dot C^\infty(X)\). Hence \(\ker A\subset\ker BA\subset\dot C^\infty\),
and similarly for \(A^*\). The mapping properties of b-operators on
weighted b-Sobolev spaces, and the Calder\'on--Fedosov formula, are inputs
(C3). The latter gives
\(\operatorname{Ind}A=\Tr(1-BA)-\Tr(1-AB)\). Finally,
\(b(B\otimes A)=BA-AB\) lifts the boundary.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Residual perturbations of the identity]\label{thm:WH}
Let \(K\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}(X)\), and suppose that for every \(H\) the family
\(a_H(\lambda)=1+I_H(K,\lambda)\) is invertible for every \(\lambda\in\C\).
Then \(A=1+K\) is Fredholm on every
\(x^\delta H^s_b\), and
\[
 \operatorname{Ind}(A)
 =\frac{i}{2\pi}\sum_H\int_{\mathbb R}\Tr_H\bigl(a_H^{-1}\partial_\lambda a_H
 \bigr)\,d\lambda
 =-\frac12\sum_H\eta_b(a_H)
 =-\sum_H\operatorname{wind}\bigl(\det a_H\bigr),
\]
where \(\eta_b(a)=\frac1{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb R}\Tr(a^{-1}\partial_\lambda a)
\,d\lambda\), and \(\operatorname{wind}\) is the winding number of the
Fredholm determinant along \(\mathbb R\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The symbol of \(A\) is \(1\), and \(a_H^{-1}-1\in B_H\) by
Lemma~\ref{lem:autoinv}. By Lemma~\ref{lem:invertible}, the inverse in
\(\Adb\) is \(1+\sum_{k\geq0}x^kl_k\) with \(l_0=a_H^{-1}-1\) and all
\(l_k\in B_H\). By Proposition~\ref{prop:structure}, there is
\(L\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}\) with this Taylor series, and then
\(B=1+L\) satisfies \(AB-1,BA-1\in\Ib\). By
Proposition~\ref{prop:fedosov} and Theorem~\ref{thm:defect},
\[
 \operatorname{Ind}A=\Tr([K,L])={}^b\Tr([K,L])
 =\sum_H\Tr_{\partial,H}(D(K)L).
\]
Since \(T(D(K))\) and \(T(L)\) contain only nonnegative powers of \(x\),
the constant term of their product is
\(i\partial_\lambda a_H\cdot(a_H^{-1}-1)\). The integral of
\(\Tr\,\partial_\lambda a_H\) vanishes. Moreover
\(\Tr(a^{-1}\partial a)=\partial_\lambda\log\det a\), and
\(\det a_H\to1\) at \(\pm\infty\), so the integral equals
\(2\pi i\,\operatorname{wind}\).
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Index \(-1\)]\label{ex:WH}
Let \(e_H\) be a rank-one projection on \(H\), and put
\(a_H(\lambda)=1+(e^{2\pi i\pi_+(\lambda)}-1)e_H\), with \(\pi_+\) as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:n1}. Then \(e^{\pm2\pi i\pi_+}-1\in\cE\),
\(a_H^{-1}=1+(e^{-2\pi i\pi_+}-1)e_H\), and \(\det a_H=e^{2\pi i\pi_+}\) winds
once. Taking \(a_{H'}=1\) for \(H'\neq H\), the operator \(1+K\) has index
\(-1\). It is not a compact perturbation of the identity on \(L^2_b\),
because its normal operator is translation invariant in \(u=\log x\). Near
\(H\), in the variable \(u\), it is a Wiener--Hopf operator on a half-line
with symbol \(a_H\). The theorem is the b-calculus form of the
Gohberg--Krein index formula \cite{GohbergKrein}.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:IF}
The index functional \(\Tr\circ\partial:\HH_1(\Ab)\to\C\) is nonzero.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For the operator of Example~\ref{ex:WH}, Proposition~\ref{prop:fedosov}
gives \(\Tr\partial[B\otimes A]=-\operatorname{Ind}(A)=1\).
\end{proof}

For operators of positive order, the residual perturbations of
Theorem~\ref{thm:WH} are replaced by general fully elliptic operators.
Their index is computed in Section~\ref{sec:zeta}
(Theorem~\ref{thm:indexgeneral}): it is the difference of the b-zeta values
of \(A^*A\) and \(AA^*\) at \(0\), minus half the sum of the b-eta invariants
\eqref{eq:etab-general} of the indicial families. In order zero the interior
term is absent, and the formula reduces to Theorem~\ref{thm:WH}
(Remark~\ref{rem:WHconsistency}).

\section{Complex powers, b-zeta functions and the index}\label{sec:zeta}

\emph{Status.} This section uses only (Q0) and (C3); its analytic inputs
are listed under (C3) in Section~\ref{subsec:status}.

This section proves the index formula for fully elliptic operators of
positive order. The arguments are those of Loya's thesis
\cite[Chs.~2--8]{LoyaThesis}, which treats manifolds with corners. We
specialize them to a manifold with boundary, where they become much
simpler, and write them in the conventions of Section~\ref{sec:bconv}. We
also replace the thesis's tempered-symbol calculus (its Chapters~3--5) by
a shorter route. The resolvent is decomposed into an explicit symbolic part,
an explicit model part at the front faces, and a remainder that is
controlled only through weighted mapping properties (Definition~\ref{def:K}).
This is all that the b-trace needs. The index identity itself is proved at
the level of operators (Proposition~\ref{prop:MS}), and the heat form of the
formula is then obtained by Mellin inversion. Throughout, \(n=\dim X\),
\(m\geq1\) is an integer, and \({}^b\Tr\) is the finite part at \(w=0\) of
\(\Tr(x^w\,\cdot\,)\), as in Section~\ref{sec:btrace}.

\subsection{The reduction lemma}\label{subsec:reduction}

We call \(A\in\Psi_b^m(X)\) \emph{fully elliptic} if it is b-elliptic and
\(I_H(A,\lambda)\) is invertible for every \(\lambda\in\C\) and every \(H\).
By Lemma~\ref{lem:invertible}, this means that \([A]\) is invertible in
\(\Ab\). We write \(A^*\) for the formal adjoint with respect to \(\mu_b\).
The kernel of \(A^*\) is \(\overline{K_A(p',p)}\). At \(\mathrm{ff}_H\) this
replaces \(s\) by \(1/s\) and exchanges \(y\) and \(y'\), so
\eqref{eq:indicial} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Iadjoint}
 I_H(A^*,\lambda)=I_H(A,\bar\lambda)^* .
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Reduction]\label{lem:reduction}
Let \(A\in\Psi_b^m(X)\), \(m\geq1\), be fully elliptic, and write
\(a_H=I_H(A,\cdot)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] There is \(B\in\Psi_b^{-m}(X)\) with \(AB-1,\ BA-1\in\Ib\). The
adjoint \(A^*\) is fully elliptic, and \(B^*A^*-1,\ A^*B^*-1\in\Ib\).
\item[(ii)] For all \(\delta,s\in\mathbb R\), the operator
\(A:x^\delta H^s_b\to x^\delta H^{s-m}_b\) is Fredholm. Its kernel is
\(N_A=\ker A\cap\dot C^\infty(X)\), and its range is the annihilator of
\(N_{A^*}=\ker A^*\cap\dot C^\infty(X)\) under the pairing
\(\int u\bar v\,\mu_b\). Hence its index is
\(\operatorname{Ind}A=\dim N_A-\dim N_{A^*}\), independent of \(\delta\) and
\(s\). This is also the index of \(A\) on \(\dot C^\infty(X)\), and it equals
\(\Tr(AB-BA)\) (Proposition~\ref{prop:fedosov}).
\item[(iii)] As an unbounded operator on \(L^2_b\) with domain \(H^m_b\),
\(A\) is closed with adjoint \(A^*\) (domain \(H^m_b\)), and its Fredholm
index is \(\operatorname{Ind}A\).
\item[(iv)] \(P=A^*A\) and \(P'=AA^*\) lie in \(\Psi_b^{2m}(X)\), are
b-elliptic with principal symbol \(|\sigma_m(A)|^2\), and are self-adjoint
on \(L^2_b\) with domain \(H^{2m}_b\). Moreover \(\ker P=N_A\) and
\(\ker P'=N_{A^*}\). For real \(\lambda\),
\[
 I_H(P,\lambda)=a_H(\lambda)^*a_H(\lambda),\qquad
 I_H(P',\lambda)=a_H(\lambda)a_H(\lambda)^*,
\]
and both are \(\geq c\,(\Delta_H+1+\lambda^2)^m\) for some \(c>0\). The
orthogonal projections \(\pi,\pi'\) onto \(N_A,N_{A^*}\) lie in \(\Ib\), and
\(P_0=P+\pi\) and \(P'_0=P'+\pi'\) are \(\geq c_0\) for some \(c_0>0\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) Let \(B_0\in\Psi_b^{-m}\) be a small-calculus parametrix, so
\(AB_0=1-R_0\) with \(R_0\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}\). The proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:invertible} inverts \(T(A)\) in
\(\bigoplus_HB'_H\Lser{x;\alpha}\), with nonnegative powers only and constant
term \(a_H^{-1}\in B'_H\) (Lemma~\ref{lem:autoinv}). Since \(B_H\) is an ideal
in \(B'_H\), the series \(T(A)^{-1}T(R_0)\) lies in
\(\bigoplus_HB_H\Lser{x;\alpha}\) and has nonnegative powers only. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:structure} it is \(T(B_1)\) for some
\(B_1\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}\). Put \(B=B_0+B_1\). Then \(AB-1\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}\),
and \(T(AB-1)=-T(R_0)+T(A)T(B_1)=0\) by Proposition~\ref{prop:structureA}.
Hence \(AB-1\in\Ib\). A left inverse modulo \(\Ib\) is built in the same way,
and it differs from \(B\) by an element of \(\Ib\). Full ellipticity of \(A^*\)
follows from \eqref{eq:Iadjoint}. The last claims follow by taking adjoints,
since \((\Ib)^*=\Ib\).

(ii) The operator \(B\) is bounded \(x^\delta H^{s-m}_b\to x^\delta H^s_b\)
(C3). The operators \(AB-1\) and \(BA-1\) have kernels in
\(\dot C^\infty(X^2)\). They therefore map \(x^\delta H^s_b\) continuously
into \(x^{\delta+1}H^{s+1}_b\), whose inclusion into \(x^\delta H^s_b\) is
compact. By Atkinson's theorem, \(A\) is Fredholm. If \(Au=0\), then
\(u=(1-BA)u\in\dot C^\infty\). The annihilator of the range in the dual
space \(x^{-\delta}H^{m-s}_b\) consists of the solutions of \(A^*v=0\). For
these, \(v=(1-B^*A^*)v\in\dot C^\infty\) by (i). Both spaces are thus
independent of \((\delta,s)\). If \(f\in\dot C^\infty\) annihilates
\(N_{A^*}\), then \(f=Au\) with \(u\in L^2_b\), and
\(u=Bf+(1-BA)u\in\dot C^\infty\). So the cokernel of
\(A:\dot C^\infty\to\dot C^\infty\) is also dual to \(N_{A^*}\). The trace
formula is the Calder\'on--Fedosov formula, as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fedosov}.

(iii) If \(u_j\to u\) in \(L^2_b\) and \(Au_j\to f\), then
\(u=Bf+(1-BA)u\in H^m_b\) and \(Au=f\). If \(v\in L^2_b\) and \(A^*v\in L^2_b\)
weakly, then \(v=B^*A^*v+(1-B^*A^*)v\in H^m_b\). For a closed Fredholm
operator, the index is \(\dim\ker A-\dim\ker A^*\), which equals
\(\operatorname{Ind}A\) by (ii).

(iv) The composite of the Fredholm maps \(A:H^{2m}_b\to H^m_b\) and
\(A^*:H^m_b\to L^2_b\) is Fredholm. The operator \(P\) is symmetric, and
\((BB^*)P-1=B(B^*A^*-1)A+(BA-1)\in\Ib\). If \(v,Pv\in L^2_b\), then
\(v=BB^*Pv-(BB^*P-1)v\in H^{2m}_b\), so \(P\) is self-adjoint. Since
\(\langle Pu,u\rangle=\|Au\|^2\), we get \(\ker P=N_A\). The indicial
identities follow from \eqref{eq:Iadjoint} and the multiplicativity of
\(I_H\). By Lemma~\ref{lem:autoinv}, \(a_H^{-1}\) is a suspended family of
order \(-m\), so \(\|(\Delta_H+1+\lambda^2)^{m/2}a_H(\lambda)^{-1}\|\leq C\)
on \(\mathbb R\) (C3). This gives the lower bound for \(a_H^*a_H\), and the
same argument with \((a_H^*)^{-1}=(a_H^{-1})^*\) gives it for
\(a_Ha_H^*\). An orthonormal basis \(\{\varphi_i\}\) of \(N_A\) lies in
\(\dot C^\infty\), so the kernel \(\sum\varphi_i(p)\overline{\varphi_i(p')}\)
of \(\pi\) lies in \(\dot C^\infty(X^2)\). Finally, \(P\) is Fredholm and
self-adjoint, so \(0\) is isolated in its spectrum. Hence \(P_0\geq c_0>0\).
The same arguments apply to \(P'\).
\end{proof}

\subsection{Weighted model operators and the b-trace}\label{subsec:Kclass}

The resolvent of \(P\) is not in the small calculus: \(I_H(P,\lambda)-\mu\)
is invertible only on a strip. The following classes contain everything
we need.

\begin{definition}\label{def:K}
Fix \(\varepsilon_0>0\) and \(0<\delta\leq\varepsilon_0/4\). Let
\(\chi_H\in C^\infty(X)\) be supported in the collar of \(H\) and equal to
\(1\) near \(H\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] \(\mathcal R^{-K}_\delta\), for \(K\geq0\), is the space of
operators that are bounded \(x^aH^s_b\to x^{a'}H^{s+K}_b\) whenever
\(|a|\leq2\delta\), \(a'=\min(a+\delta,2\delta)\), and \(|s|\leq K\).
\item[(b)] \(\mathcal M^{-k}_H\), for \(k\in\mathbb R\), is the space of
dilation-invariant operators \(M=\mathfrak m(xD_x)\) on
\(M_H=[0,\infty)\times H\). Their indicial families \(\mathfrak m\) are
holomorphic on \(|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|<\varepsilon_0\) and suspended
of order \(-k\) uniformly on \(|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|\leq3\delta\).
Orders are meant in the sense of symbol estimates, so
\(\mathcal M^{-k}_H\subset\mathcal M^{-k'}_H\) for \(k\geq k'\).
\item[(c)] \(\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\) is the space of operators \(F\) such
that, for every \(K\geq0\),
\[
 F=S_K+\sum_H\chi_HM_{K,H}\chi_H+R_K,\qquad S_K\in\Psi_b^{-k}(X),\
 M_{K,H}\in\mathcal M^{-K}_H,\ R_K\in\mathcal R^{-K}_\delta .
\]
Thus the model pieces, like the remainders, improve with \(K\); the order
\(-k\) is carried by \(S_K\).
Here \(\Psi_b^{s}\), \(s\in\C\), denotes classical b-operators of complex
order, as in \cite{SeeleyCP}, and \(-k=\operatorname{Re}s\). Holomorphic
families are defined by asking that the three pieces be holomorphic. We
set \(N_H(F)=N_H(S_K)+M_{K,H}\) and \(I_H(F,\lambda)=I_H(S_K,\lambda)
+\mathfrak m_{K,H}(\lambda)\), for \(|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|<\varepsilon_0\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:Kclass}
Let \(F\in\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\) and \(A\in\Psi_b^{m'}(X)\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] \(F\) is bounded \(x^aH^s_b\to x^aH^{s+k}_b\) for
\(|a|\leq2\delta\). Moreover \(N_H(F)\) is determined by \(F\), and so is
independent of \(K\).
\item[(ii)] \(AF\) and \(FA\) lie in \(\mathcal K^{-k+m'}_\delta\), with
\(N_H(AF)=N_H(A)N_H(F)\) and \(N_H(FA)=N_H(F)N_H(A)\). The same holds for
holomorphic families.
\item[(iii)] If \(R\in\mathcal R^{-K}_\delta\) and \(K\geq|k|\), then \(FR\)
and \(RF\) lie in \(\mathcal R^{-(K-|k|)}_\delta\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) Given \(s\), use the decomposition with \(K\geq\max(k,|s|)\). For
\(S_K\) the claim is (C3). For \(M\in\mathcal M_H^{-K}\) it follows from
Mellin--Plancherel on the line \(\operatorname{Im}\lambda=-a\), which
corresponds to the weight \(x^a\) (see \eqref{eq:indicial}). For \(R_K\) it
holds by definition. For \(u\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty)\times H)\), let
\(\tau_Tu(x,y)=u(e^Tx,y)\). This is unitary on \(L^2_b\), and
\(\tau_T^{-1}F\tau_Tu\to N_H(F)u\) as \(T\to\infty\). For \(S_K\) this follows
from the Taylor expansion at \(\mathrm{ff}_H\). For \(\chi_HM\chi_H\) the
identity is exact once \(T\) is large. For \(R_K\),
\(\|R_K\tau_Tu\|\leq C\|x^\delta\tau_Tu\|\leq Ce^{-\delta T}\).

(ii) Fix \(K'\geq0\), put \(K=K'+|m'|\), and use the decomposition of
\(F\) with this \(K\). We have \(AS_K\in\Psi_b^{-k+m'}\) by (Q0) and the
complex-order calculus (C3). Let \(\tilde\chi_H\in C^\infty(X)\) be
supported in the collar and equal to \(1\) on the support of \(\chi_H\).
Regarding \(N_H(A)\) as an operator on \(M_H\), and the collar as a subset
of \(M_H\),
\begin{align*}
 A\chi_HM\chi_H&=\chi_H\bigl(N_H(A)M\bigr)\chi_H+T_HM\chi_H,\\
 T_H&=(1-\tilde\chi_H)A\chi_H+\tilde\chi_H\bigl(A-N_H(A)\bigr)\chi_H
 +\tilde\chi_H[N_H(A),\chi_H],
\end{align*}
as one checks using \(\tilde\chi_H\chi_H=\chi_H\). The first term is a
cut-off model operator with indicial family
\(I_H(A,\lambda)\mathfrak m(\lambda)\), and
\(N_H(A)M_{K,H}\in\mathcal M_H^{-(K-m')}\subset\mathcal M_H^{-K'}\). We
claim that \(T_H\) maps \(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^{a+1}H^{t-m'}_b\) for all
\(a,t\). The kernel of \((1-\tilde\chi_H)A\chi_H\) has disjoint supports in
\(x\) and \(x'\); it vanishes near \(\mathrm{ff}\) and to infinite order at
\(\mathrm{lb}\cup\mathrm{rb}\), so the operator maps \(x^aH^t_b\) to
\(x^bH^{t'}_b\) for all \(b,t'\). By the Taylor expansion at
\(\mathrm{ff}_H\) (C3), the kernel of \(\tilde\chi_H(A-N_H(A))\chi_H\) is
\(x'\) times the kernel of an element of \(\Psi_b^{m'}\), cut off in the
same way. The kernel of \([N_H(A),\chi_H]\) is
\((\chi_H(x')-\chi_H(x))\kappa_A(0,x/x',y,y')\). It vanishes where
\(\chi_H(x)=\chi_H(x')\), in particular where both \(x,x'\) are small, and
is rapidly decreasing as \(x/x'\to0\) or \(\infty\); so after the cut-off
\(\tilde\chi_H\) it vanishes to infinite order at \(x=0\) and \(x'=0\), and
it is conormal of order \(m'\) at the diagonal. This proves the claim.
Since \(M_{K,H}\) maps \(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^aH^{t+K}_b\) for
\(|a|\leq2\delta\) and all \(t\) (Mellin--Plancherel, as in (i)),
\(T_HM_{K,H}\chi_H\) maps
\(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^{a+1}H^{t+K-m'}_b\), and so lies in
\(\mathcal R^{-K'}_\delta\). Finally, for \(|t|\leq K'\leq K\),
\(AR_K\) maps \(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^{a'}H^{t+K-m'}_b\subset
x^{a'}H^{t+K'}_b\), so \(AR_K\in\mathcal R^{-K'}_\delta\). This gives the
decomposition of \(AF\) at level \(K'\), and \(K'\) is arbitrary. The case
of \(FA\) is symmetric: one uses
\(T'_H=\chi_HA(1-\tilde\chi_H)+\chi_H(A-N_H(A))\tilde\chi_H
+[\chi_H,N_H(A)]\tilde\chi_H\), whose output decays rapidly as
\(x\to\infty\) in \(M_H\), and for \(R_KA\) the inequality
\(|t-m'|\leq K\) for \(|t|\leq K'\). (iii) follows from (i): if \(|t|\leq
K-|k|\), then \(R\) is applied in Sobolev order \(t\) or \(t+k\), both of
absolute value at most \(K\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Trace and residue]\label{lem:Ktrace}
Let \(F\in\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\) with \(k>n\).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] For \(0<\operatorname{Re}w\leq2\delta\), the operator \(x^wF\) is
trace class on \(L^2_b\), and \(\Tr(x^wF)=\int_Xx^wK_F(p,p)\,\mu_b\). The
restriction of \(K_F\) to the diagonal is continuous on \(X\). Near \(H\),
\(K_F(p,p)=f_{0,H}(y)+O(x^{\delta/2})\), where
\[
 f_{0,H}(y)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}I_H(F,\lambda)(y,y)\,d\lambda .
\]
\item[(ii)] \(\Tr(x^wF)\) extends meromorphically to
\(\operatorname{Re}w>-\delta/2\). It has at most a simple pole at \(w=0\),
with residue
\(\sum_H\frac1{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}\Tr_HI_H(F,\lambda)\,d\lambda\). The
same holds for holomorphic families \(F(w)\), with residue computed from
\(F(0)\).
\item[(iii)] Define \({}^b\Tr(F)=\operatorname{FP}_{w=0}\Tr(x^wF)\). It is
linear. It equals \(\Tr F\) if every \(N_H(F)\) vanishes, and in particular
on \(\Ib\). On \(x^{-\Z}\Psi_b^{-\infty}\cap\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\) it is the
b-trace of Section~\ref{sec:btrace}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For \(b>b'\) and \(N-N'>n/2\), the inclusion
\(x^{b}H^N_b\hookrightarrow x^{b'}H^{N'}_b\) is Hilbert--Schmidt. This is
(C3). In the collar it is the statement that
\(e^{-(b-b')|u|}\langle D\rangle^{-(N-N')}\) is Hilbert--Schmidt on
\(\mathbb R_u\times H\). By Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}(i), \(x^wF\) maps
\(x^{-\operatorname{Re}w/2}H^{-k/2}_b\) to \(x^{\operatorname{Re}w/2}H^{k/2}_b\).
So on \(L^2_b\) it is a product of a Hilbert--Schmidt map, a bounded map, and
another Hilbert--Schmidt map. It is therefore trace class, and its trace is
the integral of its continuous kernel over the diagonal.

We compute the diagonal piece by piece, taking \(K>2n\). The diagonal value
of \(S_K\) is smooth up to \(\partial X\). At \(H\) it equals the value of
its normal operator at \(s=1\), up to \(O(x)\), by the Taylor expansion at
\(\mathrm{ff}_H\). The diagonal value of \(\chi_HM\chi_H\) is exactly
\(\chi_H(x)^2\kappa_M(1,y,y)\). For \(R_K\), compose with the
Hilbert--Schmidt inclusion
\(x^{-\delta/4}H^{-K/4}_b\hookrightarrow x^{-\delta/2}H^{-K/2}_b\). The
composite is Hilbert--Schmidt into \(x^{\delta/2}H^{K/2}_b\). Its kernel
therefore lies in the Hilbert tensor product of
\(x^{\delta/2}H^{K/2}_b\) and \(x'^{\delta/4}H^{K/4}_b\), and so it is
continuous with \(|K_{R_K}(p,p')|\leq Cx(p)^{\delta/2}x(p')^{\delta/4}\).
Hence \(N_H(F)(1,y,y)=f_{0,H}(y)\) by \eqref{eq:diagvalue}, which proves (i).

For (ii), proceed as in Lemma~\ref{lem:residue}. The collar contributes
\(\int_0^\varepsilon x^{w-1}dx\int_Hf_{0,H}\nu_\partial\), plus a term that is
holomorphic for \(\operatorname{Re}w>-\delta/2\).

For (iii): if every \(N_H(F)\) vanishes, then \(f_{0,H}=0\), and
\(\Tr(x^wF)\) is holomorphic near \(0\) with value \(\Tr F\).
\end{proof}

The defect formula of Theorem~\ref{thm:defect} extends to this class. The
following statement and Theorem~\ref{thm:defect} together cover all the
cases used in these notes.

\begin{theorem}[b-trace defect]\label{thm:defectK}
Let \(A\in\Psi_b^{m'}(X)\) and \(F\in\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\) with
\(k>2(n+m'+1)\). Then \(\Tr(x^w[A,F])\) is holomorphic near \(w=0\), and
\[
 {}^b\Tr([A,F])=\sum_H\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}
 \Tr_H\bigl(\partial_\lambda I_H(A,\lambda)\,I_H(F,\lambda)\bigr)\,d\lambda .
\]
For \(A,F\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}\) this is the formula of
Theorem~\ref{thm:defect}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Lemmas~\ref{lem:Kclass} and~\ref{lem:Ktrace}, for
\(0<\operatorname{Re}w\leq2\delta\) the operators \(x^wAF\), \(x^wFA\) and
\(Ax^wF\) are trace class. Moreover \(\Tr(x^wFA)=\Tr(Ax^wF)\). Indeed, composing
\(x^wF\) with a Hilbert--Schmidt inclusion as in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:Ktrace} shows that its kernel lies in the Hilbert tensor
product of \(x^{r}H^{k/2}_b\) and \(x'^{r/2}H^{k/2-n/2-1}_b\), where
\(r=\operatorname{Re}w/2\). Since \(k/2-n/2-1>n/2+m'\), both traces are
continuous functions of the kernel for this topology. They agree on the
dense set of finite sums \(\varphi\otimes\psi\) with
\(\varphi,\psi\in\dot C^\infty\), by the definition of the transpose. As in Theorem~\ref{thm:defect},
\(\Tr(x^w[A,F])=w\Tr(x^wC(w)F)\), where \(C(w)=(A-x^{-w}Ax^w)/w\) is an
entire family in \(\Psi_b^{m'}\) with \(C(0)=D(A)\) (Lemma~\ref{lem:conj}). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}, \(C(w)F\) is a holomorphic family in
\(\mathcal K^{-k+m'}_\delta\) with
\(I_H(C(0)F,\lambda)=i\partial_\lambda I_H(A,\lambda)I_H(F,\lambda)\).
Lemma~\ref{lem:Ktrace}(ii) now gives the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Comparison with the thesis]\label{rem:defect-thesis}
Loya writes \(N_M(A)(\tau)=(x^{-i\tau}Ax^{i\tau})|_M\), the Mellin transform
\(\int s^{-i\tau}\,\cdot\,ds/s\) of the kernel at the front face
\cite[Sect.~2.4]{LoyaThesis}. This is \(I_H(A,\tau)\) in our notation, with
\(\tau=\lambda\). His Theorem~6.6 reads
\({}^b\Tr[A,B]=-\frac1{2\pi}\sum_H\int{}^b\Tr(D_\tau N_H(A)N_H(B))\,d\tau\).
The symbol \(D_\tau\) is not defined explicitly there, but his proof
requires \(N(-\log s\cdot A)=D_\tau N(A)\), and so \(D_\tau=-i\partial_\tau\).
With this, his formula is Theorem~\ref{thm:defectK}. He works with
b-half-densities. The map \(u\mapsto u\,\mu_b^{1/2}\) is unitary from
\(L^2_b\) onto \(L^2_b(X;\Omega_b^{1/2})\), so his traces, b-traces and
indices are ours.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The resolvent}\label{subsec:resolvent}

From now on, \(A\) is fully elliptic of order \(m\geq1\), and \(P\) is one
of \(A^*A\), \(AA^*\), with the notation of Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}(iv).
Write \(p_H(\lambda)=I_H(P,\lambda)\). Fix \(\theta\in(0,\pi/4]\), and
fix \(\rho>0\) smaller than half of \(c_0\) and half of the constant \(c\) of
Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}(iv). Put
\[
 \Lambda=\{\mu\neq0:|\arg\mu|\geq\theta\}\cup\{|\mu|\leq\rho\}.
\]
Let \(\Gamma\) be the boundary of \(\C\setminus\Lambda\), oriented
counterclockwise around \([c_0,\infty)\). It consists of the rays
\(re^{\pm i\theta}\), \(r\geq\rho\), and the arc
\(\rho e^{i\varphi}\), \(|\varphi|\leq\theta\).

\begin{lemma}[Indicial resolvent]\label{lem:indres}
There are \(\varepsilon_0,C>0\) such that, for
\(|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|\leq\varepsilon_0\) and \(\mu\in\Lambda\), the
operator \(p_H(\lambda)-\mu\) is invertible. Moreover
\(\|(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}\|\leq C(1+|\mu|)^{-1}\), and
\((p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}\) is holomorphic in \((\lambda,\mu)\) and
suspended of order \(-2m\), uniformly in \(\mu\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(L(\lambda)=(\Delta_H+1+(\operatorname{Re}\lambda)^2)^m\). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}(iv), for real \(\lambda\) the operator
\(p_H(\lambda)\) is self-adjoint and \(\geq cL\). For \(t\geq c\) and \(\mu\in\Lambda\) we have
\(|t-\mu|\geq c'(t+|\mu|)\). By the spectral theorem,
\(\|p_H^{s}(p_H-\mu)^{-1}\|\leq C(1+|\mu|)^{s-1}\) for \(0\leq s\leq1\). Since
\(p_H^{-1}\) is suspended of order \(-2m\) (Lemma~\ref{lem:autoinv}),
\(\|Lp_H^{-1}\|\leq C\), and by interpolation \(\|L^sp_H^{-s}\|\leq C\).
For \(\lambda\in\mathbb R\) and \(|\sigma|\leq1\), the difference
\(E=p_H(\lambda+i\sigma)-p_H(\lambda)\) is \(\sigma\) times a suspended
family of order \(2m-1\) (C3). Hence
\[
 \|E(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}\|\leq C|\sigma|\,\|L^{(2m-1)/2m}(p_H-\mu)^{-1}\|
 \leq C'|\sigma| .
\] For \(|\sigma|\leq\varepsilon_0=1/(2C')\), the factorization
\(p_H(\lambda+i\sigma)-\mu=(1+E(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1})(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)\)
and a Neumann series give the claims. The suspended estimates follow from
the parameter-dependent suspended parametrix (C3).
\end{proof}

Fix \(\varepsilon_0\) as in Lemma~\ref{lem:indres} and
\(\delta\leq\varepsilon_0/4\), and use Definition~\ref{def:K} with these
constants.

\begin{lemma}[Weighted resolvent]\label{lem:weightedres}
Shrinking \(\delta\) if necessary, for \(|a|\leq3\delta\) and
\(\mu\in\Lambda\) the operator
\(P_0-\mu:x^aH^{s+2m}_b\to x^aH^s_b\) is invertible. Moreover
\(\|(P_0-\mu)^{-1}\|_{x^aL^2_b}\leq C(1+|\mu|)^{-1}\), and the norm from
\(x^aH^s_b\) to \(x^aH^{s+2m}_b\) is bounded uniformly in \(\mu\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For \(a=0\) this is the spectral theorem, since \(P_0\geq c_0\), together
with elliptic regularity. In general, \(x^{-a}(P_0-\mu)x^a=P_0-\mu+E_a\). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:conj}, \(E_a\) is the sum of a b-operator with kernel
\((s^{-a}-1)K_P\), which vanishes on \(\Delta_b\), and an element of \(\Ib\).
So \(E_a\) is \(a\) times a family that is bounded in \(\Psi_b^{2m-1}+\Ib\)
for \(|a|\leq1\). By interpolation,
\(\|E_a(P_0-\mu)^{-1}\|\leq C|a|\,\|P_0^{(2m-1)/2m}(P_0-\mu)^{-1}\|
\leq C'|a|\). For \(3\delta C'\leq1/2\), a Neumann series inverts
\(x^{-a}(P_0-\mu)x^a\) on \(L^2_b\) for all \(\mu\in\Lambda\) at once, with
twice the norm. The Sobolev statement follows from elliptic regularity.
\end{proof}

Next comes the symbolic part, which is Seeley's construction
\cite{SeeleyCP} carried out in b-coordinates. Near \(H\) use coordinates
\((x,y)\), and b-covariables \(\zeta=(\xi,\eta)\), with \(\xi\) dual to
\(\log x\). Write the b-symbol of \(P\) as
\(p\sim\sum_{k\geq0}p_{2m-k}\), where \(p_{2m-k}\) is homogeneous of degree
\(2m-k\) for \(|\zeta|\geq1\) and smooth up to \(x=0\). Choose a smooth
\(\tilde p_{2m}\) that equals \(p_{2m}\) for \(|\zeta|\geq1\) and satisfies
\(\tilde p_{2m}\geq c_2>2\rho\) everywhere. This is possible after
shrinking \(\rho\). Put \(D^b=(xD_x,D_y)\), and define, for \(j\geq1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:seeley}
 q_{-2m}=(\tilde p_{2m}-\mu)^{-1},\qquad
 q_{-2m-j}=-q_{-2m}\sum_{\substack{k+l+|\alpha|=j\\ l<j}}\frac1{\alpha!}
 \,\partial_\zeta^\alpha p'_{2m-k}\,(D^b)^\alpha q_{-2m-l},
\end{equation}
where \(p'_{2m}=\tilde p_{2m}\) and \(p'_{2m-k}=p_{2m-k}\) for \(k\geq1\).

\begin{lemma}[Seeley symbols]\label{lem:seeley}
For \(j\geq1\),
\(q_{-2m-j}=\sum_{l=2}^{2j+1}c_{j,l}(x,y,\zeta)(\tilde p_{2m}-\mu)^{-l}\).
Here the \(c_{j,l}\) are independent of \(\mu\), smooth up to \(x=0\), and
homogeneous of degree \(2m(l-1)-j\) for \(|\zeta|\geq1\). For
\(\mu\in\Lambda\),
\[
 |\partial_{x,y}^\beta\partial_\zeta^\alpha q_{-2m-j}|\leq
 C_{\alpha\beta j}\langle\zeta\rangle^{-j-|\alpha|}
 (\langle\zeta\rangle^{2m}+|\mu|)^{-1},
\]
uniformly up to \(x=0\). Let \(Q_J(\mu)=\operatorname{Op}_b(q^{(J)}(\mu))\),
with \(q^{(J)}=\sum_{j<J}q_{-2m-j}\), be a b-quantization (small calculus,
kernel supported near \(\Delta_b\)). Then
\(r_J(\mu)=1-(P_0-\mu)Q_J(\mu)\) is a holomorphic family in
\(\Psi_b^{-J}(X)\). Its \(\Psi_b^{-J}\)-seminorms are bounded, and its
\(\Psi_b^{-J+2m}\)-seminorms are \(O((1+|\mu|)^{-1})\). The same holds for
the left parametrix \(\tilde Q_J(\mu)\), with
\(\tilde r_J(\mu)=1-\tilde Q_J(\mu)(P_0-\mu)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The structure of \(q_{-2m-j}\) follows by induction from \eqref{eq:seeley}.
Each derivative of \((\tilde p_{2m}-\mu)^{-l}\) raises \(l\) by one and
contributes a homogeneous factor of the matching degree. For
\(\mu\in\Lambda\) we have
\(|\tilde p_{2m}-\mu|\geq c(\langle\zeta\rangle^{2m}+|\mu|)\), which gives the
estimates. The recursion is the b-composition formula, which is the usual
formula in the coordinates \((\log x,y)\). So the symbol of
\((P-\mu)Q_J\) is \(1\) up to a finite sum of terms bounded by
\(\langle\zeta\rangle^{-J}\langle\zeta\rangle^{2m}(\langle\zeta\rangle^{2m}
+|\mu|)^{-1}\), together with the term \((p_{2m}-\tilde p_{2m})q\), which has
compact \(\zeta\)-support. The remainder of the composition formula
satisfies the same parameter-dependent bounds. This is the
parameter-dependent symbol calculus (C3). Finally, \(\pi Q_J\in\Ib\) is
\(O((1+|\mu|)^{-1})\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Resolvent]\label{prop:resolvent}
For every \(J>4m\) and \(\mu\in\Lambda\),
\[
 (P_0-\mu)^{-1}=Q_J(\mu)+\sum_H\chi_HM_{J,H}(\mu)\chi_H+R_J(\mu),
\]
where:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] \(M_{J,H}(\mu)\in\mathcal M_H^{-(J-2m)}\) has indicial family
\(\mathfrak m_{J,H}(\mu,\lambda)=(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}I_H(r_J(\mu),\lambda)\),
with seminorms \(O((1+|\mu|)^{-2})\);
\item[(b)] \(R_J(\mu)\in\mathcal R^{-(J-4m)}_\delta\), with norms
\(O((1+|\mu|)^{-2})\);
\item[(c)] everything is holomorphic in \(\mu\) in the interior of
\(\Lambda\) and continuous up to \(\Gamma\).
\end{enumerate}
In particular \((P_0-\mu)^{-1}\in\mathcal K^{-2m}_\delta\): given \(K\), take
\(J\geq K+4m\), so that \(M_{J,H}\in\mathcal M^{-K}_H\) and
\(R_J\in\mathcal R^{-K}_\delta\). Moreover
\(I_H((P_0-\mu)^{-1},\lambda)=(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put \(Q=Q_J+\sum_H\chi_HM_{J,H}\chi_H\). Since \(N_H(\pi)=0\) and
\((p_H-\mu)I_H(Q_J)=1-I_H(r_J)\), Lemma~\ref{lem:indres} gives
\[
 I_H(Q_J,\lambda)+\mathfrak m_{J,H}(\lambda)=(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}.
\]
Let \(r=1-(P_0-\mu)Q\). Then
\[
 r=\Bigl(r_J-\sum_H\chi_HN_H(r_J)\chi_H\Bigr)
 -\sum_H\Bigl([P,\chi_H]+\chi_H\bigl(P-N_H(P)\bigr)+\pi\chi_H\Bigr)
 M_{J,H}\chi_H,
\]
where \(\chi_H\) is inserted on both sides of \(N_H(P)\) as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}. The first bracket is \(r_J\in\Psi_b^{-J}\) minus
its cut-off normal operators; it gains a factor \(x\) at \(\mathrm{ff}\) and
maps \(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^{a+1}H^{t+J-2m}_b\) with norm
\(O((1+|\mu|)^{-1})\) by Lemma~\ref{lem:seeley}. The operator multiplying
\(M_{J,H}\) is the operator \(T_H\) of the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}(ii) for \(P\), plus \(\pi\chi_H\in\Ib\); it maps
\(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^{a+1}H^{t-2m}_b\). Since \(M_{J,H}\) has order
\(-(J-2m)\), the second sum maps \(x^aH^t_b\) to \(x^{a+1}H^{t+J-4m}_b\),
with norm \(O((1+|\mu|)^{-2})\) by (a). Hence
\(r\in\mathcal R^{-(J-4m)}_\delta\), with norms \(O((1+|\mu|)^{-1})\); the
bounds of Definition~\ref{def:K}(a) in fact hold for every Sobolev order
\(t\in\mathbb R\), not only for \(|t|\leq J-4m\). The left
parametrix \(\tilde Q\) and its error \(\tilde r\) are built in the same
way, and \(\tilde r\) has the same properties. Write \(G=(P_0-\mu)^{-1}\).
Then \(G=\tilde Q+\tilde rG\) and \(G=Q+Gr\), so
\[
 G=Q+\tilde Q\,r+\tilde r\,G\,r .
\]
Put \(R_J=\tilde Qr+\tilde rGr\). The operator \(\tilde Q\) is bounded on
\(x^aH^t_b\), \(|a|\leq2\delta\), for every \(t\), with norm
\(O((1+|\mu|)^{-1})\), by the symbol estimates of Lemma~\ref{lem:seeley}
and by (a). The operator \(G\) is bounded \(x^aH^t_b\to x^aH^{t+2m}_b\) for
\(|a|\leq3\delta\), uniformly in \(\mu\) (Lemma~\ref{lem:weightedres}).
Thus \(G\,r\) produces Sobolev orders \(t+J-2m\) outside the range
\(|t|\leq J-4m\) of Definition~\ref{def:K}(a); since \(\tilde r\) is bounded
for all Sobolev orders, the argument of Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}(iii) still
applies to \(\tilde r(Gr)\). Hence \(R_J\in\mathcal R^{-(J-4m)}_\delta\),
with the bound in (b). Holomorphy is clear from the
construction.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Complex powers and heat operators}\label{subsec:cp}

For \(\operatorname{Re}z>0\), set
\(P_0^{-z}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_\Gamma\mu^{-z}(P_0-\mu)^{-1}\,d\mu\), with the
principal branch (Figure~\ref{fig:contour}). By the spectral theorem this
agrees with the functional calculus, and \(e^{-tP_0}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_\Gamma e^{-t\mu}(P_0-\mu)^{-1}
\,d\mu\) for \(t>0\). From \eqref{eq:seeley} and Lemma~\ref{lem:seeley},
\begin{gather*}
 \frac{i}{2\pi}\int_\Gamma\mu^{-z}q_{-2m-j}\,d\mu=\sigma_{-2mz-j}(z),\qquad
 \sigma_{-2mz}(z)=\tilde p_{2m}^{-z},\\
 \sigma_{-2mz-j}(z)=\sum_{l=2}^{2j+1}c_{j,l}\binom{z+l-2}{l-1}
 \tilde p_{2m}^{-z-l+1}\quad(j\geq1).
\end{gather*}
Indeed, \((\tilde p-\mu)^{-l}=\frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{(l-1)!}\partial_{\tilde p}^{l-1}
(\tilde p-\mu)^{-1}\), and
\[
 \frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{(l-1)!}\partial_{\tilde p}^{l-1}\tilde p^{-z}
 =(-1)^{l-1}\binom{-z}{l-1}\tilde p^{-z-l+1}
 =\binom{z+l-2}{l-1}\tilde p^{-z-l+1}.
\]
These symbols are entire in \(z\)
and homogeneous of degree \(-2mz-j\) for \(|\zeta|\geq1\). Moreover
\(\sigma_{-j}(0)=0\) for \(j\geq1\), because only \(l\geq2\) occurs and
\(\binom{z+l-2}{l-1}=z(z+1)\cdots(z+l-2)/(l-1)!\) vanishes at \(z=0\); and
\(\sigma_0(0)=1\).

\begin{proposition}[Complex powers]\label{prop:cp}
For every \(J>4m\), the formula
\[
 P_0^{-z}=\operatorname{Op}_b\Bigl(\sum_{j<J}\sigma_{-2mz-j}(z)\Bigr)
 +\sum_H\chi_HM^{(z)}_{J,H}\chi_H+R^{(z)}_J,
\]
\[
 M^{(z)}_{J,H}=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_\Gamma\mu^{-z}M_{J,H}(\mu)\,d\mu,\qquad
 R^{(z)}_J=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_\Gamma\mu^{-z}R_J(\mu)\,d\mu,
\]
holds for \(\operatorname{Re}z>0\). The right-hand side is a holomorphic
family in \(\mathcal K^{-2m\operatorname{Re}z}_\delta\) on
\(\operatorname{Re}z>-1\), which defines \(P_0^{-z}\) there, with
\(P_0^{0}=1\). Its indicial family is
\(I_H(P_0^{-z},\lambda)=p_H(\lambda)^{-z}\) for real \(\lambda\), and every
seminorm of the pieces is \(O((1+|\operatorname{Im}z|)^Ne^{\theta|
\operatorname{Im}z|})\) on vertical strips. The same holds for
\(e^{-tP_0}\) and \(P_0^{-1}e^{-tP_0}\), \(t>0\), with \(\mu^{-z}\) replaced by
\(e^{-t\mu}\) and \(\mu^{-1}e^{-t\mu}\). These lie in
\(\bigcap_k\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\), with indicial families
\(e^{-tp_H}\) and \(p_H^{-1}e^{-tp_H}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Integrate Proposition~\ref{prop:resolvent} against \(\mu^{-z}\). The
pieces \(M_{J,H}\) and \(R_J\) are \(O(|\mu|^{-2})\) in
\(\mathcal M^{-(J-2m)}_H\) and \(\mathcal R^{-(J-4m)}_\delta\). By
Lemma~\ref{lem:seeley}, the terms \(q_{-2m-j}\) with \(j\geq1\) are
\(O(|\mu|^{-2})\) as symbols of order \(2m-j\), though not uniformly as
symbols of order \(-2m-j\); this is harmless, since these terms are
integrated explicitly above. So all these integrals converge for
\(\operatorname{Re}z>-1\) and are holomorphic there. Since \(J\) is
arbitrary, the right-hand side lies in
\(\mathcal K^{-2m\operatorname{Re}z}_\delta\), as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:resolvent}. The term \(j=0\) gives \(\tilde p_{2m}^{-z}\),
which is entire. On \(\Gamma\),
\(|\mu^{-z}|\leq|\mu|^{-\operatorname{Re}z}e^{\theta|\operatorname{Im}z|}\),
and \(\binom{z+l-2}{l-1}\) is a polynomial, while \(\tilde p_{2m}>0\). This gives
the growth bound. At \(z=0\), the integrands of the \(M\), \(R\) and
\(j\geq1\) terms are \(O(|\mu|^{-2})\) and holomorphic in the interior of
\(\Lambda\). Pushing \(\Gamma\) to infinity inside \(\Lambda\) shows that
these terms vanish, so \(P_0^0=\operatorname{Op}_b(1)=1\). The indicial
family is
\(\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_\Gamma\mu^{-z}(p_H(\lambda)-\mu)^{-1}\,d\mu=
p_H(\lambda)^{-z}\), by Proposition~\ref{prop:resolvent} and the spectral
theorem on \(L^2(H)\). For \(e^{-t\mu}\) the same argument applies, and the
symbols \(\frac{i}{2\pi}\int e^{-t\mu}q_{-2m-j}\,d\mu=\sum_lc_{j,l}
\frac{t^{l-1}}{(l-1)!}e^{-t\tilde p_{2m}}\) (with \(l=1\) for \(j=0\)) are
smoothing for \(t>0\). Since \(J\) is arbitrary, the operators lie in every
\(\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\).
\end{proof}

The continuation to all of \(\C\), with \(P_0^{-z}=P_0^kP_0^{-z-k}\), is
\cite[Cor.~5.7]{LoyaThesis}. We need only \(\operatorname{Re}z>-1\).

\subsection{The b-zeta function}\label{subsec:bzeta}

\begin{lemma}[Mellin transforms {\cite[Lemma~5.10]{LoyaThesis}}]\label{lem:mellin}
Let \(u\) be continuous on \((0,\infty)\) and \(O(e^{-ct})\) as
\(t\to\infty\). Suppose that, as \(t\downarrow0\),
\[
 u(t)=\sum_{i\leq I}\sum_{q=0,1}u_{i,q}t^{e_i}(\log t)^q+u_{0}+O(t^{\gamma}),
\]
with \(\gamma>0\) and \(e_i\neq0\). Then
\(v(z)=\frac1{\Gamma(z)}\int_0^\infty t^{z-1}u(t)\,dt\) extends from
\(\operatorname{Re}z\gg0\) to a meromorphic function on
\(\operatorname{Re}z>-\gamma\). It is holomorphic at \(z=0\), with
\(v(0)=u_0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The integral over \([1,\infty)\) is entire. Over \([0,1]\), the term
\(t^{e}(\log t)^q\) gives \((-1)^qq!\,(z+e)^{-q-1}\), the constant gives
\(1/z\), and the remainder is holomorphic for \(\operatorname{Re}z>-\gamma\).
Since \(1/\Gamma(z)=z+O(z^2)\), only the constant survives at \(z=0\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[b-zeta function {\cite[Def.~8.1, Thm.~8.2]{LoyaThesis}}]
\label{thm:bzeta}
The b-zeta function \(\zeta_P(z)={}^b\Tr(P_0^{-z})\), defined for
\(\operatorname{Re}z>n/2m\), extends meromorphically to
\(\operatorname{Re}z>-1\). Its only poles there are simple poles at the
points \((n-j)/2m\neq0\), \(j\in\mathbb N_0\), and it is holomorphic at
\(z=0\). Moreover, as \(t\downarrow0\),
\[
 {}^b\Tr(e^{-tP})=\sum_{0<(n-j)/2m}\Gamma\bigl(\tfrac{n-j}{2m}\bigr)
 \operatorname{Res}_{z=\frac{n-j}{2m}}\zeta_P\cdot t^{-\frac{n-j}{2m}}
 +\zeta_P(0)+O(t^{\gamma})
\]
for some \(\gamma>0\). In particular there is no \(\log t\) term at
\(t^0\), and \(\zeta_P(0)\) is the constant term of \({}^b\Tr(e^{-tP})\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:cp} and Lemma~\ref{lem:Ktrace}, \({}^b\Tr\) of
\(\chi_HM^{(z)}_{J,H}\chi_H+R^{(z)}_J\) is holomorphic on
\(\operatorname{Re}z>-1\), once \(J>4m+2n\). For the symbolic terms, the
diagonal value of \(\operatorname{Op}_b(\sigma_{-2mz-j}(z))\) at \((x,y)\) is
\((2\pi)^{-n}\int\sigma_{-2mz-j}(z;x,y,\zeta)\,d\zeta\). The integral over
\(|\zeta|\leq1\) is entire. By homogeneity, the integral over
\(|\zeta|\geq1\) is
\[
 \frac1{2mz+j-n}\int_{S^{n-1}}\sigma_{-2mz-j}(z;x,y,\omega)\,d\omega .
\]
Both are smooth up to \(x=0\), and the b-integral of a function that is
smooth up to \(\partial X\) is holomorphic in parameters. This gives
meromorphy with simple poles at \(2mz=n-j\). At \(z=0\), the only candidate
is \(j=n\), with residue proportional to \(\int_{S^{n-1}}\sigma_{-n}(0)=0\).

For the heat expansion, note first that
\(\Gamma(z)\zeta_P(z)=\int_0^\infty t^{z-1}u(t)\,dt\) for
\(\operatorname{Re}z>n/2m\), where \(u(t)={}^b\Tr(e^{-tP_0})\). This follows
by Fubini applied piecewise to the decompositions of
Proposition~\ref{prop:cp}, because the Mellin transform of
\(e^{-t\mu}\) is \(\Gamma(z)\mu^{-z}\). Next,
\(|\zeta_P(z)|\leq C(1+|\operatorname{Im}z|)^Ne^{\theta|\operatorname{Im}z|}\)
on vertical strips away from the poles, by Proposition~\ref{prop:cp} and the
explicit symbols. Since \(\theta<\pi/2\), Stirling's formula makes
\(\Gamma\zeta_P\) integrable on vertical lines. Mellin inversion gives
\(u(t)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{\operatorname{Re}z=c}\Gamma(z)\zeta_P(z)t^{-z}\,dz\).
Move the line to \(\operatorname{Re}z=-\gamma\), with
\(0<\gamma<1/2m\). The residues are those listed, plus \(\zeta_P(0)\) from
the pole of \(\Gamma\) at \(0\), and the new integral is \(O(t^\gamma)\).
Finally, \(e^{-tP}=e^{-tP_0}+(1-e^{-t})\pi\), and \({}^b\Tr\pi=\Tr\pi\) by
Lemma~\ref{lem:Ktrace}(iii). The extra term \((1-e^{-t})\dim\ker P\) has no
constant term.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The b-eta function and the index theorem}\label{subsec:indexthm}

For each \(H\), write \(a=a_H\), and define for
\(\operatorname{Re}z>(n-1)/2m\) and \(t>0\)
\begin{equation}\label{eq:etaH}
 \eta_H(z)=\frac1{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb R}\Tr_H\bigl(\partial_\lambda a\,
 a^{-1}(aa^*)^{-z}\bigr)\,d\lambda,\qquad
 \eta_H(t)=\frac1{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb R}\Tr_H\bigl(\partial_\lambda a\,
 a^{-1}e^{-taa^*}\bigr)\,d\lambda .
\end{equation}
The integrals converge absolutely: \(\partial_\lambda a\,a^{-1}\) is
suspended of order \(-1\), and \((aa^*)^{-z}\) of order \(-2m\operatorname{Re}z\).
Put \(\eta(z)=\sum_H\eta_H(z)\) and \(\eta(t)=\sum_H\eta_H(t)\). Let
\(h(t)={}^b\Tr(e^{-tA^*A})-{}^b\Tr(e^{-tAA^*})\) and
\(h_0(t)={}^b\Tr(e^{-tP_0})-{}^b\Tr(e^{-tP'_0})\), with
\(P=A^*A\) and \(P'=AA^*\).

\begin{proposition}[McKean--Singer--Melrose identity]\label{prop:MS}
For \(t>0\) and \(\operatorname{Re}z>n/2m\),
\[
 e^{-t}\operatorname{Ind}A=h_0(t)-\tfrac12\eta(t),\qquad
 \operatorname{Ind}A=h(t)-\tfrac12\eta(t),
\]
\[
 \operatorname{Ind}A=\zeta_{A^*A}(z)-\zeta_{AA^*}(z)-\tfrac12\eta(z).
\]
These are \cite[Lemma~7.3, Lemma~8.3 and (89)]{LoyaThesis}, with the sign
of the boundary term corrected as explained in Remark~\ref{rem:thesis-sign}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(f\) be one of \(\mu^{-z-1}\), \(\mu^{-1}e^{-t\mu}\). Polar decomposition
gives \(g(AA^*)A=Ag(A^*A)\) on \((\ker A)^\perp\) for bounded Borel \(g\). Using
\(\pi'A=0\), this yields
\[
 P_0f(P_0)=\pi\,f(1)+A^*f(P'_0)A,\qquad
 P'_0f(P'_0)=\pi'f(1)+AA^*f(P'_0).
\]
Put \(G=A^*f(P'_0)\). By Proposition~\ref{prop:cp} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}, \(G\in\mathcal K^{-k}_\delta\) with
\(k=2m\operatorname{Re}z+m\) for \(f=\mu^{-z-1}\), and with every \(k\) for
\(f=\mu^{-1}e^{-t\mu}\). For real \(\lambda\)
\[
 I_H(G,\lambda)=a^*f(aa^*)=a^{-1}(aa^*)f(aa^*).
\]
Since \(f(1)=1\) for \(f=\mu^{-z-1}\) and \(f(1)=e^{-t}\) for
\(f=\mu^{-1}e^{-t\mu}\), Lemma~\ref{lem:Ktrace}(iii) and
Theorem~\ref{thm:defectK} give
\begin{align*}
 {}^b\Tr(P_0f(P_0))-{}^b\Tr(P'_0f(P'_0))&=f(1)\operatorname{Ind}A
 -{}^b\Tr[A,G]\\
 &=f(1)\operatorname{Ind}A-\frac{i}{2\pi}\sum_H\int_{\mathbb R}
 \Tr_H\bigl(\partial_\lambda a\,a^{-1}(aa^*)f(aa^*)\bigr)\,d\lambda .
\end{align*}
Since \(-\frac{i}{2\pi}=\frac12\cdot\frac1{\pi i}\), this is the first
identity, and also the third for \(\operatorname{Re}z\) so large that
\(k>2(n+m+1)\). Both sides of the third identity are holomorphic for
\(\operatorname{Re}z>n/2m\) (Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta} and \eqref{eq:etaH}), so
it holds there by analytic continuation. The second follows from \(h_0(t)=h(t)+(e^{-t}-1)
\operatorname{Ind}A\), because \(e^{-tP_0}=e^{-tP}+(e^{-t}-1)\pi\).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[b-eta function {\cite[Lemma~8.4]{LoyaThesis}}]\label{lem:etaH}
Each \(\eta_H(z)\) extends meromorphically to \(\operatorname{Re}z>-1\), with
at most a simple pole at \(z=0\). The sum \(\eta(z)\) is holomorphic at
\(z=0\), so the residues at \(0\) add up to zero. Moreover
\(\eta(0)\) is the constant term of \(\eta(t)\) as \(t\downarrow0\), and
\(\eta(t)\) has no \(\log t\) term at \(t^0\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(F(z)=D(A)A^*(P'_0)^{-z-1}\). Here \(D(A)\in\Psi_b^{m-1}(X)\) by
Lemma~\ref{lem:conj}, and \(A^*\in\Psi_b^m(X)\). By
Proposition~\ref{prop:cp} and Lemma~\ref{lem:Kclass}(ii), applied to
\((P'_0)^{-z-1}\in\mathcal K^{-2m(\operatorname{Re}z+1)}_\delta\), this is a
holomorphic family in
\(\mathcal K^{-1-2m\operatorname{Re}z}_\delta\). For real \(\lambda\), its
indicial family is \(i\partial_\lambda a\,a^{-1}(aa^*)^{-z}\). Hence, for
\(\operatorname{Re}z>(n-1)/2m\), Lemma~\ref{lem:Ktrace}(i) gives
\(\eta_H(z)=-2\int_Hf_{0,H}(z,y)\,\nu_\partial\), where \(f_{0,H}(z,\cdot)\)
is the value at \(H\) of the diagonal of \(F(z)\). Write \(F(z)\) as in
Definition~\ref{def:K}, using Proposition~\ref{prop:cp} and the composition
formula. The symbolic part has symbol
\(\sum_j\tau_{-1-2mz-j}(z)\), homogeneous of degree \(-1-2mz-j\) for
\(|\zeta|\geq1\). As in Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta}, its diagonal at \(x=0\) is
meromorphic with simple poles at \(2mz=n-1-j\). This includes a possible
pole at \(z=0\) from \(j=n-1\). The remaining pieces are holomorphic. The
sum \(\eta(z)\) equals \(2(\zeta_{A^*A}(z)-\zeta_{AA^*}(z)-\operatorname{Ind}A)\)
by Proposition~\ref{prop:MS}, and this is holomorphic at \(0\) by
Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta}. Finally,
\(\eta(t)=2(h_0(t)-e^{-t}\operatorname{Ind}A)\), and Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta}
gives its expansion. Its constant term is
\(2(\zeta_{A^*A}(0)-\zeta_{AA^*}(0)-\operatorname{Ind}A)=\eta(0)\). The same
conclusion follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:mellin}, since
\(\frac1{\Gamma(z)}\int_0^\infty t^{z-1}\eta(t)\,dt=\eta(z)\) for
\(\operatorname{Re}z\gg0\) by Fubini.
\end{proof}

For a family \(a=I_H(A,\cdot)\) as above, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:etab-general}
 \eta_b(a)=\operatorname{FP}_{z=0}\frac1{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb R}
 \Tr_H\bigl(\partial_\lambda a\,a^{-1}(aa^*)^{-z}\bigr)\,d\lambda .
\end{equation}
If \(a=1+k\) with \(k\in B_H\), as in Theorem~\ref{thm:WH}, the integrand is
trace class and integrable for every \(z\), and is entire in \(z\). So
\eqref{eq:etab-general} agrees with the definition used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:WH}.

\begin{theorem}[General index formula]\label{thm:indexgeneral}
Let \(A\in\Psi_b^m(X)\), \(m\geq1\), be fully elliptic, and let
\(\operatorname{Ind}A\) be its index (Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}): on any
\(x^\delta H^s_b\), on \(L^2_b\), or on \(\dot C^\infty(X)\), equal to
\(\Tr(AB-BA)\). Then
\[
 \operatorname{Ind}A=\zeta_{A^*A}(0)-\zeta_{AA^*}(0)
 -\frac12\sum_H\eta_b\bigl(I_H(A)\bigr).
\]
Here \(\zeta_P(0)\) is the value at \(0\) of the b-zeta function of
Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta}, which is the constant term of \({}^b\Tr(e^{-tP})\)
as \(t\downarrow0\). The b-eta invariants are those of
\eqref{eq:etab-general}, and their sum is the constant term of \(\eta(t)\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Take the value at \(z=0\) in Proposition~\ref{prop:MS}, using
Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta} and Lemma~\ref{lem:etaH}. The finite parts of the
\(\eta_H\) add up to the value of \(\eta\) at \(0\), because the residues
cancel.
\end{proof}

This is the codimension-one case of \cite[Thm.~8.6]{LoyaThesis}, which
extends Piazza's formula \cite{Piazza} to manifolds with corners. Here are
the hypotheses there. \(X\) is a compact manifold with corners;
\(A\in\Psi^m_{b,os}(X,\Omega_b^{1/2})\), where ``os'' means one-step
polyhomogeneous, that is, classical; \(m>0\) is real; and
\(A:H^m_b\to L^2_b\) is Fredholm. By \cite[Cor.~2.6]{LoyaThesis}, the last
condition holds exactly when \(A\) is elliptic and every \(N_H(A)(\tau)\),
\(\tau\in\mathbb R\), is invertible. A fully elliptic \(A\) satisfies all of
these, by Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}. Full ellipticity is used above only in
Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}: to put \(\pi,\pi'\) in \(\Ib\), to get the lower
bound in (iv), and to get the parametrix \(B\) and the identification of
the index with \(\Tr(AB-BA)\).

\begin{remark}[Conventions and a sign in the thesis]\label{rem:thesis-sign}
In the notation of Remark~\ref{rem:defect-thesis}, \(N_H(A)(\tau)=a(\tau)\),
\(D_\tau=-i\partial_\tau\), and \(N_H(AA^*)(\tau)=a(\tau)a(\tau)^*\) for real
\(\tau\). The thesis defines the b-eta function of a face \(M\) by
\cite[(82)]{LoyaThesis}:
\[
 {}^b\eta_M(t)=\frac2{2\pi}\int_t^\infty\int_{\mathbb R}
 {}^b\Tr\bigl(D_\tau N(A)N(A^*)N(e^{-sAA^*})\bigr)\,d\tau\,ds .
\]
Since \(N(A^*)e^{-saa^*}=-a^{-1}\partial_se^{-saa^*}\) and
\(e^{-saa^*}\to0\) as \(s\to\infty\), the inner \(s\)-integral is
\(+D_\tau a\,a^{-1}e^{-taa^*}\). The last line of the computation preceding
\cite[Lemma~7.3]{LoyaThesis} has exactly this sign. So (82) equals our
\(\eta_H(t)\). The second expression \cite[(83)]{LoyaThesis},
\(-\frac2{2\pi}\int{}^b\Tr(D_\tau N(A)N(A)^{-1}N(e^{-tAA^*}))\,d\tau\), has the
opposite sign, and so equals \(-\eta_H(t)\). The thesis carries (83) into
Lemma~7.4, Lemma~8.3, \((89)\) and the displayed formula for
\({}^b\eta_M(z)\) in Theorem~8.6. In our notation that formula is
\(-\eta_H(z)\), which would give
\(\operatorname{Ind}A=\zeta-\zeta+\frac12\eta\). This is wrong. The correct
statement is Theorem~\ref{thm:indexgeneral}, as the explicit check in
Example~\ref{ex:signcheck} confirms. The results that use (82) directly,
namely \cite[Lemma~7.9, Thms.~7.10, 8.7, Cor.~8.8]{LoyaThesis}, have the
correct sign: they reduce to the Atiyah--Patodi--Singer formula. Two
smaller slips occur in \cite[Cor.~5.12, Thm.~8.2]{LoyaThesis}. The residue
at a pole \(z_k=(n-k)/m\) coming from \(t^{-z_k}\) is
\(\int\gamma_k/\Gamma(z_k)\), not \(\int\gamma_k/\Gamma(-z_k)\). The logarithmic
terms give poles at \(z_k\in-\mathbb N\), not at \(z_k\in\mathbb N\). Both
follow from \cite[Lemma~5.10]{LoyaThesis}, which is stated correctly.
Finally, \cite[Lemma~8.4]{LoyaThesis} asserts holomorphy at \(0\) only for
the sum over faces, and the individual \({}^b\eta_M(z)\) may have poles
there. This is why \eqref{eq:etab-general} uses a finite part.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Sign check]\label{ex:signcheck}
Let \(X=[0,1]\), with the b-structure at both ends, and let
\(u=\log(x/(1-x))\), so that \(\mu_b=du\). Put
\(A=\partial_u+\tanh u\), a b-differential operator. It is Fredholm on
\(L^2_b\), but not fully elliptic: \(a_0(-i)=0\) below. So this example
checks the sign of the identity of Proposition~\ref{prop:MS} in the
generality of the thesis. For this \(A\), the conclusions of
Lemma~\ref{lem:reduction}(iv) still hold, except that \(\pi,\pi'\) lie only
in \(\mathcal R^{-K}_\delta\) for every \(K\) (the kernel of \(A\) is
\(O(x)\)). With this change, the proofs of Propositions~\ref{prop:resolvent},
\ref{prop:cp} and~\ref{prop:MS} go through unchanged. The kernel of \(A\) is
spanned by \(1/\cosh u\in L^2\), and \(\ker A^*\), spanned by \(\cosh u\),
is trivial in \(L^2\). Hence \(\operatorname{Ind}A=1\).
Figure~\ref{fig:signcheck} shows the data of this example.

Near \(x=0\) we have \(\partial_u=x(1-x)\partial_x\), and \(\tanh u\to-1\), so
\(a_0(\lambda)=i\lambda-1\) by \eqref{eq:IxDx}. Near \(x=1\), with
\(x'=1-x\), we have \(\partial_u\to-x'\partial_{x'}\), and so
\(a_1(\lambda)=-i\lambda+1\). For \(a(\lambda)=\pm i\lambda+c\), \(c\) real,
\[
 \frac1{\pi i}\int_{\mathbb R}\frac{\pm i}{\pm i\lambda+c}
 (\lambda^2+c^2)^{-z}\,d\lambda
 =\pm\frac{c}{|c|^{1+2z}}\,
 \frac{\Gamma(z+\frac12)}{\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(z+1)} ,
\]
since the odd part integrates to zero. So \(\eta_b(i\lambda-1)=-1\) and
\(\eta_b(-i\lambda+1)=-1\), and the boundary term is
\(-\frac12(-1-1)=+1\). The interior term vanishes. Indeed,
\(A^*A=-\partial_u^2+1-2\operatorname{sech}^2u\) and \(AA^*=-\partial_u^2+1\)
have the same normal operators. So
\(h(t)=\Tr(e^{-tA^*A}-e^{-tAA^*})\), which has the small-time expansion
\((4\pi t)^{-1/2}e^{-t}\sum_{k\geq1}c_kt^k\), with \(c_1=-\int(-2
\operatorname{sech}^2u)\,du=4\). This expansion has no constant term. Hence
\(\operatorname{Ind}A=0+1=1\), as it should be. The printed form of
\cite[Thm.~8.6]{LoyaThesis} would give \(-1\). The order-zero case gives the
same sign: see Remark~\ref{rem:WHconsistency}.
\end{example}

\(\operatorname{sech}u\) (red), which is in \(L^2\), while
\(\ker A^*\) is spanned by \(\cosh u\notin L^2\); so

\begin{remark}[Order zero and Theorem~\ref{thm:WH}]\label{rem:WHconsistency}
This remark is a consistency check, and we only indicate the argument.
For \(A=1+K\) as in Theorem~\ref{thm:WH}, \(P_0-1\in\Psi_b^{-\infty}+\Ib\).
The operator \(P_0\) is bounded with spectrum in a compact subset of
\((0,\infty)\), so a compact contour may be used, and
\[
 P_0^{-z}-1=-\frac{i}{2\pi}\oint\mu^{-z}(1-\mu)^{-1}(P_0-1)(P_0-\mu)^{-1}\,
 d\mu
\]
is an entire family of residual operators. Since \(K\) is residual, the
trace-class conditions in the defect formula hold automatically. The
proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:MS} then gives
\[
 {}^b\Tr(P_0^{-z}-1)-{}^b\Tr(P_0'^{-z}-1)=\operatorname{Ind}A
 -\frac{i}{2\pi}\sum_H\int_{\mathbb R}\Tr_H\bigl(\partial_\lambda a\,a^{-1}
 (aa^*)^{-z}\bigr)\,d\lambda .
\]
Here \({}^b\Tr[A,G]={}^b\Tr[K,G]\), and \(K\) is residual. At \(z=0\) the
left side vanishes, which is Theorem~\ref{thm:WH} with the same sign. In
order zero there is no interior term.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Residue form; not proved here]\label{rem:residue-form}
On closed manifolds, \(\zeta_{A^*A}(0)-\zeta_{AA^*}(0)\) can be rewritten as
a Wodzicki residue built from the symbols of \(A\) and a parametrix, the
``Atiyah--Singer term'' \(\operatorname{AS}(A)\). An earlier version of this
section stated the b-analogue. It requires the meromorphy of
\(\Tr(x^w[A,B]Q^{-\tau})\) in two variables, and we have not proved it.
Theorem~\ref{thm:indexgeneral} is stated, and proved, with the zeta values.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Relation to the companion notes]\label{rem:companion-heat}
The companion notes \cite{Notes} quote the b-heat kernel theorem only for
product-type generalized b-Laplacians, through the parabolic heat
calculus. The operators \(e^{-tA^*A}\) used here have \(A\)
pseudodifferential and are not of product type, so they lie outside that
theorem. We do not use a heat calculus. The heat operators are contour
integrals of the resolvent (Proposition~\ref{prop:cp}), and their small-time
behaviour on the diagonal is read off from the b-zeta function by Mellin
inversion (Theorem~\ref{thm:bzeta}). The b-trace defect formula of
\cite{Notes} is the case of Theorem~\ref{thm:defectK} with both operators
in the full calculus.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
Moroianu and Nistor relate index and homology for manifolds with boundary
\cite{MoroianuNistor}. Liu and Ma refine eta invariants through
differential \(K\)-theory \cite{LMlocalization}. Mazzeo and Melrose treat
the fibred-boundary analogue of the calculus \cite{MazzeoMelrose}.
\end{remark}
